Brothers,
Below is a note from TE Sean Lucas about a racial reconciliation resolution that will go through the
Overtures Committee this year. I see this as a step in the right direction. Glad to hear your thoughts
on it as well.

I will set up a discussion page on the website ASAP. The actual resolution is attached.

JK




I've attached the resolution for you--it is a resolution on racial reconciliation that finally recognizes our
covenantal and generational involvement in and complicity with racial injustice during the Civil Rights era.
We started working on this a year ago, but pulled back in order to get more feedback from African
American pastors and others. After circulating it through key black PCA pastors through RAAN and getting
feedback, we decided we need to move forward with this.

I've been emailing Roy Taylor about this in the run-up. He intends to give this to Overtures Committee on
Monday and have them work on a response presuming that the Assembly will give a 2/3 vote on Tuesday
night to refer it as new business. It will also show up in the OC briefing materials that will be going out to
them through Sharefile so that it can be seen in advance as well. So, this will be getting out there.

We are working on a talking point sheet for this resolution. Some of the issues that I've already
encountered in sharing this with our session and presbytery (as well as input from Roy and others):


        The spirituality of the church: going back and confessing our sins and failures during this era
         involves us in political and not religious/theological issues
      Covenantal connection: not everyone in the PCA today was involved in these issues; only involves
         old PCUS churches, and not all of them; what about the Korean Presbyterians in the PCA--are we
         confessing for them too?
      Previous statements on this: the RPCES had a statement on this as well as the 1966 PCUS
         General Assembly; don't those statements cover this already?
      The historical proof: the overture doesn't contain "the historical proof"--how do we know these
         things are so?
      Outright disagreement: we do not believe that these charges or claims occurred; this is an
         assumption of "white guilt" that is unnecessary
I'm sure there are other things that will come up, but these are the things that I've encountered already
in talking about this with others. Once I write responses, I'll pass it along.

I wanted you to be aware, but I thought it might be good to alert those to whom you are connected that
this is coming. I'm passing the word as well to other key folks to whom I'm connected so that this doesn't
take people completely by surprise. Let me know your thoughts, questions, or other issues I need to
consider as we run up to GA.

Thankful for you and excited for what God is doing in the new plant already. My brother-in-law lives in
Westerville; is that close at all to Hillyard?

Best,
Sean
Personal Resolution on Civil Rights Remembrance
Whereas, last year and this year mark significant anniversaries in the Civil Rights movement: 2014 was
the sixtieth anniversary of the United States Supreme Court decision Brown v. Board of Education and the
fiftieth anniversary of the Civil Rights Act and Freedom Summer, and 2015 was the fiftieth anniversary of
the Voting Rights Act and the Selma-to-Montgomery March; and


Whereas, many of our conservative Presbyterian churches at the time not only failed to support the Civil
Rights movement, but actively worked against racial reconciliation in both church and society; and


Whereas, the 30th General Assembly adopted a resolution on racial reconciliation that confessed its
covenantal, generational, heinous sins connected with unbiblical forms of servitude, but failed to deal
with the covenantal, generational, heinous sins committed during the much more recent Civil Rights era
(cf. Daniel 9:4-11); and


Whereas, the 32nd General Assembly adopted a pastoral letter on “the Gospel and Race” that was
produced under the oversight of our Mission to North America committee, but that also failed to
acknowledge the lack of solidarity with African Americans which many of our churches displayed during
the Civil Rights era; and


Whereas, our denomination’s continued unwillingness to speak truthfully about our failure to seek justice
and to love mercy during the Civil Rights era significantly hinders present-day efforts for reconciliation
with our African American brothers and sisters; and


Whereas, God has once more given our denomination a gracious providential opportunity to show the
beauty, grace and power of the gospel of Jesus Christ by showing Christ-like love and compassion
towards the greater African American community;


Be it therefore resolved, that the 43rd General Assembly of the Presbyterian Church in America does
recognize and confess our church’s covenantal and generational involvement in and complicity with racial
injustice inside and outside of our churches during the Civil Rights period; and


Be it further resolved, that this General Assembly recommit ourselves to the task of truth and
reconciliation with our African American brothers and sisters for the glory of God and the furtherance of
the Gospel; and
Be it finally resolved, that the General Assembly urges the congregations of the Presbyterian Church in
America to confess their own particular sins and failures as may be appropriate and to seek to further
truth and reconciliation for the Gospel’s sake within their own local communities.


TE Sean M. Lucas
TE J. Ligon Duncan III

43rd Assembly Introduction:

After a couple of years of relatively peaceful assemblies I think the 43rd assembly will follow the recent
pattern: there are a couple of contentious issues but no wide-ranging divisive ones. These are moments
where there will be significant debate and about which we need to pray!



Overture 7 would compel testimony in judicial cases. This will be heartily debated. Here’s the link to the
Partnership discussion thus far: http://thenationalpartnership.com/overture-7/



Overtures 2 and 9 will create a study committee to deal with the oft-taken exception to our standards
regarding the Sabbath: http://thenationalpartnership.com/new-blog-1/



The Review of Presbytery Records will give a majority report (where most citations are reviewed without
incident) and four minority reports. Some have provided a rationale for voting in the minority on three of the
reports and that summary is posted on the website here: https://james-kessler.squarespace.com/rpr-
minority-reports/. I would appreciate the help of some who can provide more in-depth analysis, especially
those who voted in the minority at the RPR meeting. Please add your understanding of the issues in the
comments section. Additionally there will be a fourth minority report I and others in this group would oppose
allowing Westminster Presbytery to maintain “pre-fail” standards for ordinands. Essentially that presbytery
has created additional standards, like required 6-day 24-hour creation, in order to receive a man for
Ordination or transfer. The majority report cited the presbytery for this and there is a minority report
contending that the practice is appropriate.

These reports will be hotly debated due to the fact that the Review of Presbytery Records has become the
location of surrogate battles over orthodoxy in the PCA. Paedocommunion, suspected federal vision, how
confessionalism works, etc., the lines that exist in our denomination show most clearly when RPR meets.



A Personal Resolution on Civil Rights Remembrance will work its way through the Overtures Committee.
Though I don’t suspect it will be hotly contested, there will probably be rhetoric you hear from some corners
about the propriety of one generation repenting for another generation’s sins, why are we getting bogged
down in racial stuff again, etc. More on this later but I am an enthusiastic supporter of this resolution, and any
resolution, that recognizes the need for the church to speak more courageously about our past sins in order
that we might speak in good faith and hope about both the present and the future. Text of the resolution can
be seen here: http://thenationalpartnership.com/resolution-racial-reconciliation/
The Nominating Committee reports Thursday at 10.15am – it is a fixed item so regardless of how ahead or
behind we are the report will take place at that hour. There will be a stiff contest yet again for the SJC
nominations (see the RPR report above for the reason why). I will send a separate email on the issue with
advice.



Times to make sure you’re on the floor. (non pub-hours) You’ll get an email alert from me.

Wednesday:

130pm – Review of Presbytery Records. Get yourself back from lunch!

Thursday:

10.15am – Nominating Committee Report.

3.30pm – Overtures Committee Report – there’s no telling if we’ll be on time for this report or not. I’ll update
you during the week on the time for this report.



I will email you with any urgent votes or needs for your involvement. Please be sure your email settings are
not set on ‘digest’, which will hold any incoming Partnership emails until the end of the day. You’ll miss any
urgent communication. I believe the vast majority of you have your settings on ‘all emails’, which is what we
want.

The Overtures Committee will meet Monday afternoon until their business is done. They will reconvene
Tuesday morning and Wednesday if necessary. For those of you who are unfamiliar with the way the OC
works: each presbytery may send 2 commissioners (one RE and one TE). The OC acts as a sort of senate to
deal with overtures sent to the assembly. They may amend the overtures before them (overtures may not be
amended from the floor of the General Assembly) and their report may contain minority opinions. When the
Overtures report comes to the floor of the Assembly (slated for Thursday at 3.30pm) the majority and
potential minority reports are presented then debated. Again, the overtures in these reports may not be
amended.



My advice is provided as an additional resource for your preparation but I want to emphasize once again
that participation in the National Partnership does not imply your conformity to the opinions expressed. I
have formed my opinion through the opinions offered on the website, my conversations with polity experts in
our denomination and, of course, my own biases! I hope the following is helpful for you. Since we will not be
holding up our placards this year (unless there is a problem with the electronic voting) you will have a hard
time just following the crowd!



Overtures of note:

advocating for this study committee (attached). Further discussion on the website
here: http://thenationalpartnership.com/new-blog-1/



#3 (Change Bapitsmal Vow). Advice: NO. As currently written there seems to be no compelling reason to
change the wording. I leave open the possibility that the OC may amend the wording enough to make
something helpful out of it, but as currently written I don’t think we gain
anything. http://thenationalpartnership.com/new-blog/
#7 (Compel testimony). Advice: NO. This will be the most hotly debated overture. I take TE Scott Seaton’s
view of the protection we currently enjoy. Please note that fellow partnership member RE EJ Nusbaum (also a
member of the SJC) has written an able opinion in favor of the overture. A great discussion
online:http://thenationalpartnership.com/overture-7/



#9 (Study Committee regarding Sabbath). Advice YES. See advice on #2 above.

denomination. Pray with me that the discussion will be courageously honest and
compassionate. http://thenationalpartnership.com/resolution-racial-reconciliation/



                                        Recreation on the Sabbath


    It is common in exams in TVP for candidates to take exception to WCF chapter 21,
section 8 and LC questions 117 and 119 and SC questions 60 and 61. TVP routinely
approves these exceptions regarding recreation on the Sabbath without discussion. In
fact, taking exception to the Standards’ prohibition of recreation on the Sabbath seems
to have become the default position of the PCA.
    With this reality it seems to us that it is time to consider amending the Standards
regarding recreation on the Sabbath. We recognize that this is not a process to be
entered into without careful thought and most importantly without clear Scriptural
guidance. But it is our belief that it is Scripture itself that needs to be heard. Objecting to
recreation on the Sabbath cannot come from merely not liking the prohibition. We
recognize that often we must do what we might naturally desire not to do because of our
faithfulness to God’s Word. At times we must be willing to stand against our culture
because of our commitment to Scripture as God’s rule of faith and life. Is the prohibition
of recreation on the Sabbath to be included in this resistance to the prevailing winds
that characterize our culture? We are not convinced that it is. And we base this on what
we believe Scripture commands. At the same time we wish to be clear that our proposal
is not intended to lessen our appreciation of the Confession and Catechisms but is rather
a call for greater confessional integrity.
   This short paper will seek to address two critical questions: Is the prohibition of
recreation on the Sabbath expressly set down in Scripture? Is recreation forbidden on
the Sabbath by good and necessary consequence from Scripture? One will recognize that
this is the framework given by the WCF for understanding “the whole counsel of God”
(WCF chapter 1, section 6: “The whole counsel of God, concerning all things necessary
for his own glory, man’s salvation, faith, and life, is either expressly set down in
Scripture, or by good and necessary consequence may be deduced from Scripture:
unto which nothing at any time is to added, whether by new revelations of the Spirit,
or traditions of men. Nevertheless we acknowledge the inward illumination of the
Spirit of God to be necessary for the saving understanding of such things as are
revealed in the Word; and that there are some circumstances concerning the worship
of God, and government of the Church, common to human actions and societies, which
are to be ordered by the light of nature and Christian prudence, according to the
general rules of the Word, which are always to be observed”).
   It is our belief that the case for or against recreation on the Sabbath must be made
from Scripture. For that reason we do not enter into the historical situation that gave
rise to the Assembly dealing with this specific matter. We are aware of the historical
context of the Westminster Assembly and the positive influence of Nicolas Bownd’s 1595
treatise on the Sabbath and the negative influence of King James’ Book of Sports issued
in 1617 and1633. However informative this history may be in demonstrating the cultural
influences on the Westminster Assembly, the final appeal must be to God’s Word which
the Assembly believed they were interpreting correctly.
   While considering whether Scripture forbids recreation on the Sabbath it is
necessary to remember the positive nature of the fourth commandment. In Mt. 22.36-
40 Christ summarized the first table of the Law with the words “You shall love the Lord
your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your mind” (see also LC
question 102). As we approach the fourth commandment we are being given instruction
as to how we are to demonstrate our love to God as we keep the Sabbath.


   First – is recreation on the Sabbath forbidden by divine command? The Sabbath
command in Ex. 20.8-11 and Dt. 5.12-15 begins with the instruction to remember the
Sabbath day, to keep it holy. This opening sentence gives the general instruction that
God’s people are to celebrate and call to memory the seventh day that it may be
separated and set apart to God. A major difference in the Sabbath day from the other six
days is the restriction on work on the Sabbath. In the use of ʿaa bad (to labor) and
mlaa ʾka (occupation, work), the commandment has in view what the Confession
phrases as worldly employments. ʿaa bad is the term used in Gen. 2.5,15; 3.23; 4.2,12 to
speak of tilling the ground and in Ex. 5.18 to do the work of making bricks. These
examples are consistent with the use of ʿaa bad throughout the Old Testament, and it is
plain that the word is limited to work in the sense of physical labor. The use of mlaa ʾka
falls into the same category as can be seen from its use in passages such as Gen. 2.2; Jer.
18.3; and Jonah 1.8. Based on the terms used in the fourth commandment it is labor not
activities in general, that is prohibited on the Sabbath.
  But what about Isaiah 58.13 which the Standards use as a proof text to forbid
recreation: “If you turn back your foot from the Sabbath, from doing your pleasure on
my holy day, and call the Sabbath a delight and the holy day of the Lord honorable; if
you honor it, not going your own ways, or seeking your own pleasure or talking idly”
(ESV)? Does this not clearly forbid any form of recreation on the Sabbath? The context
of the chapter is God’s critique of formalism in Israel’s worship with specific focus on
fasting and Sabbath keeping. Both fasting and Sabbath are to be a “delight” or
“pleasure” (the Hebrew reading, ee pes). It must be emphasized that ee pes is not
referring to fun and games but to the things that we take pleasure in or are a delight to
us. We see this usage in passages such as Is. 54.12 (walls of precious stone); Mal. 3.12
(all the nations will call you blessed for you will be a land of delight); Job 31.12 (if I
have withheld anything that the poor desired); Is. 44.28 (I am the Lord Who says of
Cyrus, he is my shepherd and he shall fulfill all my purpose); Is. 53.10 (the will of the
Lord shall prosper in his hand); Prov. 31.13 (she seeks wool and flax and works with
willing hands); Eccl. 3.17 (God will judge the righteous and the wicked, for He has
appointed a time for every matter and every work); Is. 58.3 (behold, in the day of your
fast you seek your own pleasure, and oppress all your workers). It is apparent from the
wide range of usage that the meaning of ee pes cannot be limited to recreation and there
is good reason to question whether this is the correct understanding of the term in Is.
58.13. This is particularly true in light of its use in Is. 58.3 which points to a meaning
associated with work not recreation. Given the semantic range of ee pes, its usage in
passages such as Prov. 31.13, Eccl. 3.17 and its context in Is. 58, it is likely that the idea
of “pursuing one’s business” would make more sense in Is. 58.13. In summary, from the
words used in the Sabbath command and in Is. 58 we are not convinced that recreation
is forbidden by divine command.
  Second - Is recreation on the Sabbath forbidden by good and necessary consequence?
Is. 58.13 states that God will bless those who turn back from doing their pleasure, their
delight, their will on the Sabbath. Obviously that does not include the pleasure of
worship and serving God. It does at least mean that the Sabbath is not a day to do with
as we please. Does that preclude recreation? To consider this question it is helpful to
think about the purpose of the Sabbath as a gift of God – a sign of His goodness. Jesus
taught in Mk. 2.27 that the Sabbath is for our benefit and as such is a sign of God’s
grace. The reaction of the Pharisees here is quite instructive. They “watched Jesus, to
see whether He would heal him (a man with a withered hand) on the Sabbath, so that
they might accuse Him” (Mk. 3.2). The Pharisees interpreted the Sabbath in terms of
Law and had lost sight of the Sabbath as a sign of God’s grace. Jesus’ anger at them (Mk.
3.5) is understandable. This helps us understand the Sabbath command – God is
interested in a proper heart attitude over against a mere external obedience. In this light
Is. 58.13, while consistent with the fourth commandment, carries us further along so
that it is not only a matter of refraining from our work on the Sabbath but also not
viewing the day as a “free” day in which we may pursue our “pleasures” – whatever
those pleasures may be. This understanding is undoubtedly behind the Standards’
prohibition of recreation. But we believe that it prohibits too much. What is condemned
is recreation or any other thing occupying the focus of the day. We can and must say that
the Sabbath is not to be devoted to recreation, but that is a far different statement than
saying that all recreation is to be prohibited. Is. 58.13 is a control on our Sabbath
observance reminding us that we are to devote the day to the worship and enjoyment of
our Creator and Redeemer not our own pursuits.
   How do we properly worship and enjoy God on the Sabbath? We follow the example
that God set forth on the seventh day of creation as Ex. 20.11 instructs us – God rested.
We know that God did not rest because He was physically tired. This directs us away
from seeing the Sabbath as being intended solely for the purpose of providing a time of
physical rest, although since God knows how we are made (Ps. 103.14), this purpose
certainly is included. But the significance of God’s rest on the seventh day can be better
understood from Ex. 31.17 – “on the seventh day He rested and was refreshed.” In Ex.
23.12 we read: “Six days you shall do your work, but on the seventh day you shall rest;
that your ox and your donkey may have rest, and the son of your servant woman, and
the alien, may be refreshed. The Hebrew root for refresh is naa pass , the common term
for breath or to breathe. The idea of its use in the Sabbath command is that God finished
His creation work and then took a breath. Surely it was not to “catch His breath.” Most
likely the idea is that on the seventh day God ceased from His work of creation and was
then able to look at, contemplate, and enjoy His creative activity. To follow this pattern
means that on one day of the week we can rest and be refreshed in body and soul
because God is the Creator. We can rest from our fear and concern for our needs
because it is God, our Creator, who will sustain us and supply what we need. We see this
at work in the wilderness wanderings when God commanded that no manna be collected
on the Sabbath as He would supply enough on the sixth day (Ex. 16.22f). We also see
this in the command that the land be given a Sabbath rest with no reaping or gathering
every seventh year (Lev. 25.1-7). How could God command such a radical agricultural
policy? What answer was there for those who asked “what shall we eat in the seventh
year if we may not sow or gather in our crops?” (Lev. 25.20) God replies: “I will
command my blessing on you in the sixth year, so that it will produce a crop sufficient
for three years” (Lev. 25.21). God promises this as the Creator and Sustainer of life.
Relating this to Ex. 20.10, we can rest and be refreshed on the Sabbath because God is
our Creator and Sustainer. We do not need to fear for our own needs when God
commands that we labor only six days of the week. Instead we can give worship to God
who supplies our needs and breathe – be refreshed. Does this allow for recreation? We
believe that it does as part of our refreshment on the Sabbath.
  The Sabbath command is to be understood not only in light of creation but also in
light of God’s redemption of His people (Dt. 5.15). Here we are to observe the Sabbath
with reference to God as our Redeemer. God brought His people out of bondage in
Egypt into the rest of the promised land which foreshadowed the Sabbath rest (Heb. 4).
As is the case in acknowledging God as Creator, keeping the Sabbath in light of God as
Redeemer is an exercise of faith. The Sabbath is to be a time set aside in which God is
worshipped as Redeemer; it is to be a time of resting in His redemptive work. The cares
and concerns of this world hold no terror for us. We do not have to spend seven days
laboring to insure our existence and safety. We look to God’s redemptive work in which
He secures life for us. Since God takes this responsibility upon Himself we are called to
rest in Him, delight in Him, and joyfully and freely give worship to Him. Can this
include recreation? Recreation as an exercise in freedom certainly seems appropriate to
the Sabbath day.
  Our conclusion is that Scripture does not either expressly prohibit recreation on the
Sabbath nor does it forbid it by good and necessary consequence. But we trust that in
saying this we have made clear that we do not believe that the Sabbath is a time for
doing as we please. Any activity on the Sabbath must be examined as to its consistency
with the Sabbath as a sign of response to God as our Creator and Redeemer. Recreation
is not always a good thing for it can be abused and misused as can all God’s good gifts.
We recognize that far too often recreation is pursued as an end in itself. Jacques Ellul’s
insightful quote is to the point: “Obviously the Christian does not understand the holy
day if on it he prefers to head for the beach or the ski slopes. It is not just that he has
been badly taught or does not need rest. But he will not take up his freedom. He is not
acting like a man who is freed in Christ. Nor has he discovered that prayer and worship
in fact refresh him far more effectively than eight hours of play” (The Ethics of Freedom,
p. 130). There can also be a problem with recreation that does not lend itself to
enjoyment of God or fellowship with other Christians. A Sunday “couch potato”
watching sports all day would be a case in point. If a given recreational activity distorts
or detracts from the significance of the day which God in His goodness has given us,
then that type of recreation should be stopped. But that is far different from prohibiting
all recreation on the Sabbath. From our study of Scripture we humbly believe that the
Westminster fathers got this wrong and we propose that all references to the forbidding
of recreation on the Sabbath in the Standards be stricken.

General Assembly NOTE #3: important schedule notes and
RPR advice

to TheNP@googlegroups.com

Tuesday Schedule notes:
    1. The opening worship service: Outgoing moderator Bryan Chapell preaching.
    2. Following the opening worship will be the opening session of the General Assembly. RE Jim Wert will
        be nominated. I am not sure of his opponent. I’ve heard good things from everyone I’ve asked about
        Jim. When you’re not sure of a man’s moderating skill, temperament is all you’ve got to go on. I plan
        to vote for Jim.
    3. Following the election of the moderator we have what will be the most important business of the
        assembly: TE Sean Lucas and TE Ligon Duncan will be presenting their resolution on racial
        reconciliation (Link: ). The resolution will be read and then requires a 2/3 majority to send the
        resolution to the Overtures Committee for them to deal with Wednesday morning. Please be sure to
        be present tonight and vote (even if you must be late to an after session party). There will be
        opposition, unfortunately in my opinion, to this overture. Pray and vote.
BACKGROUND AND ADVICE ON RPR REPORT
The RPR report will be given around 1.30pm on Wednesday. I cited this report as one of the primary things
you want to engage on the floor of the Assembly. It will most likely follow lunch Wednesday so you’ll need to
hustle back. I will send an email when the time of the report is fairly certain.

RPR will present both a majority report (the report passed by their committee during their meeting in May)
and FOUR minority reports. Here are the basic facts related to the minority reports:
Eastern Pennsylvania - By a vote of 26-22, the RPR committee adopted a recommendation to judge, as
Unsatisfactory, Eastern PA’s 2014 updated response to an exception of substance citation relating to an exam
from November 2011 where Presbytery sustained an ordination exam in which the candidate stated a
difference with Larger Catechism 177 on the admissibility of covenant children to the Lord’s Supper. The
Minority intends to recommend the GA judge Eastern PA’s updated response as Satisfactory. There are 20
signers of this MR and RE Howie Donahoe will present it.

Susquehanna Valley - By a vote of 29-22, the RPR committee adopted a recommendation to cite
Susquehanna with an exception of substance for sustaining a BCO 13-6 transfer exam of a PCA minister who
expressed a difference with Larger Catechism 177 and his view, but not practice, on the admissibility of
covenant children to the Lord’s Supper (TE Sam Andreades, transferring in from Metro NY in Jan 2014). There
are 18 signers of this MR and RE Howie Donahoe will present it.

Philadelphia - By a vote of 26-25, the RPR committee adopted a recommendation to
cite Philadelphia with an exception of substance for sustaining a Feb 2014 ordination
exam in which a candidate (Chris Currie) stated he was not 100% sure the New
Testament “itself teaches a universal prohibition of women eldership.” The Minority
intends to recommend the GA not cite Philadelphia with an exception of substance
for their judgment on this matter. There are 13 signers on this MR and TE Tim Lecroy
will present it.


Westminster - will be presented by RE Flynt Jones from Central Carollina (session clerk at Christ
Covenant). The RPR committee (in the majority report) adopted a recommendation to cite Westminster
Presbytery with an exception of substance for adopting a policy prohibiting anyone, even a candidate, from
even being examined by Westminster Presbytery, unless they hold a particular views about Adam and
creation – including 6 day, 24-hour creation.
_____

Advice:
I intend to vote to sustain the Eastern Pennsylvannia and Susquehanna Valley minority reports. Eastern
Pennsylvannia and Susquehanna Valley are of the same type: these are citations for ministers who have a
stated view in conflict with LC 177 (the admissibility of covenant children to the Lord’s Supper). They have a
view, but not practice, of Paedocommunion. While the vast majority in the PCA do not share a
Paedocommunion view we have allowed, as a matter of common practice, men to have the view but not to
practice such. Additionally there is a method for dealing with an Elder who spreads heretical views – the man
should be charged with an offense rather than being cited through the review process. Using RPR for this
process is an error of category confusion. You can hammer a nail with a hand grenade, but I wouldn’t
recommend it.

I intend to vote to sustain the Philadelphia minority report. This one is a little more of a nuanced issue. I
believe this issue arose out of a fiercely divided discussion both at the presbytery and committee level. It
sounds terrifying: a man denies the NT teaching on the ordination of women?! But I feel confident that is not
in fact what happened. Everything I’ve heard and read tells me that this is the case of an ordinand not
speaking the language of his presbytery. For a man to talk about “100% certainty” is in some cases a matter of
temperament: some people are more comfortable with generalities and others are painfully specific. Rather
than a doctrinal difference I believe this was a philosophical and epistemological one (can we say 100% about
any issue where further scholarship and the work of the spirit may affect our interpretive grid?). I don’t think
this is a high-stakes issue. While I will support the minority report I do not believe there will be significant
damage either way.

I will vote against the Westminster minority report. I am concerned about any presbytery adding a “pre-fail”
super-confessional view for potential ordinands. Confessionalism cuts both ways: it both maintains on our
historic doctrinal standards and protects us against endless exclusionary inventions.

General Assembly: FINAL NOTE

to TheNP@googlegroups.com

Brothers,

Work done: (we were of course not unanimous in our opinions here – I am speaking from my perspective)
     1. Reaffirmed the proper role of the RPR by pushing back on alleged “exceptions of substance” in the
         majority report. Two of the reports we targeted were affirmed. This is a good outcome for the future
         peace of our denomination. We reaffirmed the bounds of good-faith subscription in the process.
         Thankful for men that were willing to go to microphones and push: Omar Ortiz and Joel St. Clair,
         Bruce O’Neil, Tim LeCroy, many others.
     2. Defeated an overture that would have opened the door further to unnecessary prosecution of men
         for alleged doctrinal offenses. It is still my view that gross heresy does not need the testimony of the
         accused to be discovered. On a 3% margin the overture was defeated.
     3. Confirmed some great nominees for permanent committees in the PCA
     4. Lodged a protest on the last night of the GA that allowed our men to speak to the issue of racial
         reconciliation. I was proud of the men that stood in there and spoke boldly, for Bobby Griffith, Kevin
         Twit, Mike Sloan, and Jon Price for not letting us go before we had a chance to talk as one big
         denomination about our complicated history.
Work undone:
     1. While we were attentive Monday-Wednesday, we fell apart as a group Thursday. There were 350 less
         voters in the room and those voters were the broad center of the denomination. We lost nearly every
         significant vote and all of our floor nominees we recommended were defeated. Lots of reasons for
         this that we’ll address next year.
     2. The racial reconciliation overture will require more from the NP this year. After the NP summer
         sabbatical I will outline the process we will engage to get the right overture to the floor of the GA.
An exhausting week of meetings and fast-walking ended with more than a whimper last night, though to what
great end we rang the bell is not yet clear. Please bear with me as I thank our Lord with all of you for the
move of the Spirit last night. How do I know it was the work of the Spirit? Because men do not lay down
power willingly. They do not tarry in prayer willingly, they do not vote anonymously to extend debate willingly.
Jesus will never grow tired of reminding us that he is the head of His church. Thank you to those that could
show up last night and did. Thank you Jesus for Jim Baird, patriarch for our beautiful and small denomination,
who waved his old arms all over the place and pointed his finger for the sake of calling himself to repentance.
Sins of omission burn from the inside out and thank God I had a chance to watch that holy thing happen right
in front of my eyes. I thank of so many of you who labored long hours in the OC (specifically David Richter
who had an impossible task of pushing for a reconciliation overture that would not frustrate a new and
growing African American leadership team) for some way to answer the burden we all carried to address the
continuing degradation of our African American brothers and our inability to deal with injustice in a way that
honors God. Thank you for making speeches all week, and voting when it was time to vote, and reading all of
the emails. There is much more work yet to do.

Thanks again for your hard work and energy on behalf of the Kingdom, and our little corner of it.
JK
2016

Partnership NOTE

                                        Skip to first unread message



james
<james@newcityhilliard.org>

Jan 13, 2016, 4:11:45 PM

to TheNP@googlegroups.com

[The following communication is confidential and may not be shared without the permission of the sender]
 Partnership Website: http://thenationalpartnership.com (password: ‘rev223’)


Brothers,
After a long sabbatical following last year’s GA, we are ready to dig back in and prepare for Mobile. A couple of
reminders as we get started:
-This group exists as a way to resource one another. We want you to feel prepared for the Assembly and engaged in
its work on the Presbytery level.
-This group does not tell you how to vote. Even if I/we make recommendations please remember that we are
grateful for diversity. We are looking for unity, not uniformity.
-Being a part of the National Partnership means that you are committing to participation in the business of our
denomination. We will be about the logistics of denominational health; we aren’t a visioning committee.
-The NP also creates a place to have a conversation in confidence; nothing here is reproduced and blogged or
whatever. Our discussion boards are places to stretch and reason together. Please feel free to use them.


Outlook for Mobile:
I believe this assembly will be primarily concerned with addressing the Overture on Racial Reconciliation. A little
history:
          -TE Sean Lucas and TE Ligon Duncan co-authored a resolution perfected over several months to last year’s
GA
          -The overtures committee voted unanimously (after very vigorous debate) to table the issue until this
year’s GA. The debate was, in my opinion, at times discouraging and cynical. Those seeking to pass the overture
pressed the issue ably but in the             end a coalition of thoughtful Overtures Committee members, in
consultation with some of our African American Teaching Elders pushed to table the issue in order to use 2015-
2016 as a period of refinement for the overture, discussion and                          repentance for the
denomination.
          -There were hundreds who protested the tabling of the overture due to concern that delay on the matter
of addressing our culpability as a denomination was harmful to our witness. In an unprecedented period of prayer
and confession the                                    Assembly invested Thursday evening in dealing with this issue
as a body – while still tabling the amendment. It was a beautiful time.
Following the Assembly a group consisting of TE Sean Lucas and some of our African American brothers worked to
further perfect an amendment with concrete action steps. This new version was circulated for feedback then
revised and was first passed by the Missouri Presbytery (Overture 4). There is a competing amendment from
Heartland Presbytery, overture 1, that seeks more specificity in steps but softens language of the
covenant/collective responsibility of today’s denomination in the silence of the Presbyterian church during the Civil
Rights Movement. Essentially, there is disagreement in the two versions regarding whether or not we can/should
publicly repent for the sins of our fathers.


There will be quite a debate at this year’s Overtures Committee. Since there will be more than one version of the
amendment, discussion will center on a way to combine the two, or find a consensus document. It is my opinion
that any consensus efforts will leave us with a weakened version. For those seeking to pass the revised (TE Lucas)
version of the amendment can find it here: http://www.pcaac.org/wp-content/uploads/2015/10/Overture-4-
Missouri-Confession-Commitment.pdf
Chesapeake has also passed this version. I think the most effective ways to move this through the Overtures
Committee are:
     1. Move the amendment without any alterations (avoiding a third option at the OC) if you think you can. This
         is the strongest move, but requires a unified presbytery.
     2. Move an overture to affirm overture four (similar to what we did regarding ‘preventing child sexual abuse
         in the church’). This avoids the headache of drafting and parsing language on your presbytery floor. This
         allows for a more divided presbytery to in effect “draft” an amendment without having to agree on all of
         the language.
I have opened a discussion section on the website and would love for you all to share your
thinking: http://thenationalpartnership.com/new-blog/2016/1/13/overture-4-from-missouri-presbytery-to-oc-
confession-of-sin-and-commitment-to-racial-and-ethnic-diversity




1. News
-Travel to Mobile is expensive! I would suggest travel to Pensacola then renting a car for the 1-hour drive to
Mobile.
-Overture 1 through Heartland Presbytery is an attempt to deal with the Racial Reconciliation overture (Overtures
4, 6) with practical steps without accepting culpability for the “sins of our fathers.” I think it would benefit all of us
to have a robust discussion of the two versions on this website. As I say above, I prefer overture 4/6’s
understanding of covenantal responsibility. Here is the link to discussion about Overture 1:
-There are already 3 overtures dealing with the operation of judicial commissions, unsurprising given the tense
debates over SJC decisions the past couple of years.
-Overture 2 is the latest in a series of helpful corrections to the way judicial process is outlined in our manuals.
Here’s a link to the page, should you wish to discuss it: http://www.pcaac.org/wp-
content/uploads/2015/09/Overture-2-Pacific-NW-OMSJC-18.12.pdf
-Overture 3 seeks to allow minority reports in judicial decisions:http://www.pcaac.org/wp-
content/uploads/2015/11/Overture3ChesapeakeBCO15-3.pdf
-Overture 5 aims to give the Assembly the right to instruct the SJC in particular cases: http://www.pcaac.org/wp-
content/uploads/2015/11/Overture-5-Pittsburgh-BCO-15-5.a-RAO-17-1.pdf


2. Action items:
-January is probably the month for your presbytery meeting. It may be time to stand for election to committees!
-Begin to familiarize yourself with the two competing forms of the Racial Reconciliation Overture
-Move in your presbyteries to either adopt an identical form of Overture 4 (if you are in favor of this form, as I am)
or affirm overture 4.
-Join the Overtures Committee. We have several unused seats on this committee every year. Please also note that it
is not rude or inappropriate to stand for election to the committee in opposition to a sitting member. Presbyteries
simply vote on the person they would like to send. This is the group that will debate the Overtures that come
before the assembly, as a sort of senate. Please consider joining this important work.
-Join a permanent committee. Much important work is done on both the Nominating Committee and Review of
Presbytery Records (I hope to join RPR this year and have served 3 years on the NC). Note on permanent
committee participation below.


PERMANENT COMMITTEES:
We’ve mentioned the value of participation in the Review of Presbytery Records committee and the Nominating
Committee as two key ways you can do your part to preserve the health of our denomination. Participation in the
NP means, at a minimum, that you will be involved in helping to fill committee spots from your presbytery. Either
volunteer yourself for open slots on RPR and the Nominating Committee (The NC recommends members for the
permanent committees and the SJC to the floor of the General Assembly) or find a friend in your presbytery to
volunteer. Your Winter meeting is the likely point for nominations to these committees. The Nominating Committee
is a one-day meeting in Atlanta, Saturday April 2nd. Your travel and accommodation (you will likely travel Friday
night for this meeting) is reimbursed by the denomination. Review of Presbytery Records meets for a few days
during one week in May, your travel and accommodation is reimbursed for this meeting.


3. Calendar:
January-February: Presbytery meetings with elections to permanent and special committees
April 2: The Nominating Committee meets in Atlanta
June 20-24: The Assembly meets in Mobile

Brothers,
There will be no Donald Trump related news in this update.
As we move toward the assembly, please be in prayer for the social media discussion in the weeks leading to the
meeting. The discussion on racial reconciliation has been extraordinarily positive, but the disagreements have been
sharp, particularly related to “generational sins.” No one likes admitting the sins of the daddies and grandfathers. I
am of the opinion that any resolution ignoring covenantal, generational responsibility will be a mistake. This is the
primary difference between the overtures to the assembly and will be the subject of continued blogs, etc. So lets
pray that we will have the advantage of wisdom in Mobile.

There will be quite a debate at this year’s Overtures Committee. For those seeking to pass the revised (TE Lucas)
version of the amendment can find it here: http://www.pcaac.org/wp-content/uploads/2015/10/Overture-4-
Missouri-Confession-Commitment.pdf
Chesapeake has also passed this version. I think the most effective ways to move this through the Overtures
Committee are:
     1. Move the amendment without any alterations (avoiding a third option at the OC) if you think you can. This
         is the strongest move, but requires a unified presbytery.
     2. Move an overture to affirm overture four (similar to what we did regarding ‘preventing child sexual abuse
         in the church’). This avoids the headache of drafting and parsing language on your presbytery floor. This
         allows for a more divided presbytery to in effect “draft” an amendment without having to agree on all of
         the language.
I have opened a discussion section on the website and would love for you all to share your
thinking: http://thenationalpartnership.com/new-blog/2016/1/13/overture-4-from-missouri-presbytery-to-oc-
confession-of-sin-and-commitment-to-racial-and-ethnic-diversity




1. News
-There are 7 Overtures affirming Overture 4, “Confession of Sin and Commitment to Racial and Ethnic Diversity,”
and several “near versions” of the overture.
-There are already 3 overtures dealing with the operation of judicial commissions, unsurprising given the tense
debates over SJC decisions the past couple of years.
-Overture 5 aims to give the Assembly the right to instruct the SJC in particular cases. This is a reaction to the TE
Leithart case in which the CCB maintained its scope to oversee the SJC only on matters of adherence to the
committee manual. Basically the CCB makes sure SJC traffic obeys the speed limit and traffic lights, not where the
traffic is headed. I’m in favor of keeping it that way, and from what I’ve heard SJC members and the chairman of the
CCB are in favor of maintaining the committee’s current scope as well. Here’s more from this particular
overture: http://www.pcaac.org/wp-content/uploads/2015/11/Overture-5-Pittsburgh-BCO-15-5.a-RAO-17-1.pdf


2. Action items:
-Please let us know if you are doing committee work at this Assembly. Particularly if you are on the Overtures
committee.
-You can register for the Overtures committee up to the day it meets, the Monday of the assembly. Would you
consider filling an empty spot in your presbytery? The committee debate will be sharp and particularly important
this year.
-Move in your presbyteries to either adopt an identical form of Overture 4 (if you are in favor of this form, as I am)
or affirm overture 4.




3. Calendar:
April 2: The Nominating Committee meets in Atlanta
May: RPR meets in Atlanta
June 20-24: The Assembly meets in Mobile

Brothers,
Three weeks out from the assembly and we need to start resourcing one another. There are two ways we will do
that:
     1. A discussion regarding the main overtures hosted on the Partnership website
     2. Regular updates during the assembly with alerts for critical votes and summaries.
There will be quite a debate at this year’s Overtures Committee about the Confession of Sin and Commitment to
Racial and Ethnic Diversity overture, the revised version of last year’s Lucas-Duncan motion. There is a place to
discuss the overture, and the alternate forms passed by other presbyteries
here: http://thenationalpartnership.com/new-blog/2016/1/13/overture-4-from-missouri-presbytery-to-oc-
confession-of-sin-and-commitment-to-racial-and-ethnic-diversity
To further aid you in understanding the differences between these overtures TE Joel St. Clair (Potomac) has
compiled the attached chart. It is a very helpful resource for you as you prepare for the floor discussion.


1. News
-There are 20 presbyteries affirming the basic form of Overture 4, “Confession of Sin and Commitment to Racial
and Ethnic Diversity,” either by an overture of affirmation or by submitting a very “near version” of the overture.
There are also several forms that drop important elements like covenantal responsibility (the idea that all of us bear
the burden of confession for the sins of churches to whom we are connected through our denomination).
-There are already multiple overtures dealing with the operation of judicial commissions, unsurprising given the
tense debates over SJC decisions the past couple of years.
-One of those judicial-case-focused overtures is an attempt to again pass a BCO amendment removing the right
against self-incrimination for witnesses in judicial cases; that is, compelling the testimony of the accused. This was
defeated on the floor of the assembly last year. TE Scott Seaton has given a helpful critique of this proposed
amendment, and I will again vote against its passage this year. http://theaquilareport.com/the-pca-and-the-right-
against-self-incrimination-against-overture-14-at-the-pca-general-assembly/
-Overture 5 aims to give the Assembly the right to instruct the SJC in particular cases. This is a reaction to the TE
Leithart case in which the CCB maintained its scope to oversee the SJC only on matters of adherence to the
committee manual. Basically the CCB makes sure SJC traffic obeys the speed limit and traffic lights, not where the
traffic is headed. I’m in favor of keeping it that way, and from what I’ve heard SJC members and the chairman of the
CCB are in favor of maintaining the committee’s current scope as well. Here’s more from this particular
overture: http://www.pcaac.org/wp-content/uploads/2015/11/Overture-5-Pittsburgh-BCO-15-5.a-RAO-17-1.pdf


-In non-Overture news, this from TE Cal Boroughs:
The Administrative Committee is bringing a major recommendation to this GA, a recommendation that was
developed by the Cooperative Ministries Committee. Recommendation 3 is for a study committee on the issue of
women serving in the ministry of the church. Particular attention is to be given to the question of the meaning of
ordination, the nature and function of the office of deacon, and "clarification on the ordination or commissioning
of deacons/deaconesses."

This is a study that needs to be done. Some of us believe that women should be ordained as deacons. Others of us
disagree and believe that the BCO gets it right. Some have developed other creative forms for women
serving/assisting in diaconal functions that are more or less ambiguous about our present position.

The Administrative Committee report is docketed for 1:30 PM Thursday afternoon along with other COC and
informational reports. For many this is boring and they prefer to be somewhere else. I understand but urge you to
not miss this.

If you are on the Administrative Committee of Commissioners be prepared for this recommendation.

You may want to review what the RPCES worked on before Joining and
Receiving: http://www.pcahistory.org/findingaids/rpces/docsynod/390.html#m

The final action by a close vote was as follows:

                               An Affirmation Concerning Biblical "Ordination"
"Ordination" as seen in the New Testament is the solemn setting apart of an individual to represent the Church
of Jesus Christ.

In accordance with Scripture the special officers of the church (teaching and ruling elders, and deacons) are set
apart to their tasks by means of distinctive, formal "ordination" or "setting apart" ceremonies. Presbyterian, and
we believe biblical, church polity understands that ordination ceremonies do not convey special grace nor the
ability to be an officer. The Holy Spirit give gifts to believers. When both the body and the individual recognize
the Spirit's gifts to and calling of that individual to a special office, that person is "set apart" or "ordained." The
ceremony is an acknowledgment of the gifts and calling of the Holy Spirit.

That which essentially distinguishes the ordination of an elder from that of a deacon is neither the form nor the
ceremony, but rather the specific task to which the individual is set apart.

An elder is set apart to serve the community by teaching, exhorting, and ruling. He exercises a disciplinary
authority. The deacon serves the community as its representative in matters primarily of physical need. The
deacon does possess delegated authority to represent the church in deeds of mercy. Such serving authority is
not to be confused with the teaching and disciplinary authority of elders. The deacon serves under the direction
of the session (elders) and, in the capacity of deacon, disciplines no one.
In this day of general ignorance as to the meaning of "ordination," Reformed Presbyterian elders should be
especially careful to educate their congregations as to the nature and meaning of biblical ordination.
ACTION: Synod adopted the affirmation. The report was continued in the afternoon meetings (see below). Synod
recessed for lunch. Rev. Robert Hamilton led in prayer.

                                           ROLE OF WOMEN, continued

At 2:20, Dr. Hurley made the following motion on behalf of the majority:
                                                  RECOMMENDATION:
That some women, full of the Holy Spirit and with appropriate gifts from him, may be called of God to serve the
body of Christ as deacons and that women so gifted and called may be set apart (ordained) to the office of
deacons. After the majority position was debated, Mr. Miladin moved the following minority position:
We affirm in the absence of any compelling biblical evidence to support the ordination of women to the special
office of deacon, that this office be limited to qualified men. At the same time acknowledging that the Scriptures
contain many examples of serving women, and also mention "women" in 1 Timothy 3: 11, suggesting a possible
third class of serving people in conjunction with elders and deacons, we affirm the right of a local church to have a
separate body or board of women (which may be called deaconesses), auxiliary and subordinate to the deacons, to
be compatible with Scripture.
                                                      FINAL ACTION:
After lengthy debate and several amendments, the minority position read as follows was adopted at 10:15 p.m.
Negative votes were recorded by W. Zumbach and R. Freiwald.
We affirm in the absence of any compelling biblical evidence to support the ordination of women to the special
office of deacon, that this office be limited to qualified men. At the same time acknowledging that the Scriptures
contain many examples of women who serve, we affirm the right of a local church to have a separate body of
unordained women who may be called deaconesses.

If you are interested in discussion Recommendation 3 further we have opened a discussion on the confidential
partnership website: http://thenationalpartnership.com/admin-committee-rec-3-on-women-in-the-office-
of-deacon/

We will have multiple General Assembly notes. This first note is for the purpose of giving a basic schedule and
overview of the assembly with key points in the schedule.


Quick reminder:
The advice shared in this and other notes should not be seen as coercive. We are seeking unity rather than
uniformity. The Partnership Website is one place for you to speak back or please feel free to email me if you feel the
advice given is unhelpful, incomplete, or erroneous. The purpose of the Partnership is to resource one another. When
advice is shared it is seeking to help, not coerce.


OVERVIEW
This year’s assembly continues a theme from the last few years: we are still attempting to figure out how to function
as a growing denomination swimming in strong cultural waters. There are four primary issues about which most
debate in the assembly will be concerned:

    1. The need for racial reconciliation in light of sins suffered in America’s Civil Rights era, and the current
       effects of that sin
    2. A proposed Study Committee regarding the role of women in ministry
    3. Overture 14 which again seeks to compel ordained elders to provide testimony in judicial cases (removing
          the so-called right agains self-incrimination)
    4. Various overtures seeking to further define/limit the power and role of the Standing Judicial Commission of
          the PCA
Racial Reconciliation
Overture 4 was the basis for much of what you see in the Overtures regarding RR. While Overture 4 was the basis, it
has been replaced on the first day of pre-assembly meetings through the work of the Overture Committee. There is
now a modified version of the overture, Overture 43, that will come to the floor. Some key language was softened
(particularly regarding the terms ‘covenantal and generational sin’) but the finished product I believe provides much
for which to be thankful. There will undoubtedly be some who prefer both more and less from this overture; I would
regard this as the first wobbly steps of recognition and repentance. More to come.


Study Committee
The Admin Committee of Commissioners we received a recommendation from the CMC through the Admin
Committee establishing a study committee on the role of women, particularly as it regards women
strengthening the work of committees in both the local and broader church. This is my twelfth General
Assembly and I have never seen such a stacked committee of commissioners. The composition of the
committee was staunchly conservative, and the debate was not at all representative of the broader
denomination. The commissioners substituted the recommendation for a study committee with a
recommendation that one NOT be appointed. The vote was 31-7. I strongly support such a committee. The
discussion was lively, albeit at some points bewildering. The committee found itself at once suggesting that
there is nothing unsettled about the issue and decrying the diversity of uninformed opinions about the
subject. So while bemoaning the need for more learning on the subject they vehemently denied the
opportunity for such a study. We will have the opportunity from the floor to adopt the original motion to
establish the study committee or to sustain the CoC’s rejection of it.

Further notes will address in greater detail overtures and nominees.

A word regarding participation, copied from a friend:
Please remind folks, probably often, and especially Thursday morning, that Committee elections will be an
Order of the Day at 10:15 am Thursday (report of Nom Comm). It follows the SJC report, which is preceded by
the CCB report. I'd hate to see a surprise floor nominee get on an important committee.

As an example, perhaps guys need to be reminded that in 2013 in Greenville, Grover Gunn, as a floor
nominee, defeated the Nom Comm nominee Cal Boroughs from TN Valley by just 53 votes - 394-341 (+12 for
D. Shuffield = 747 votes case). Since there were 1,327 registered Commissioners, that means only 56% voted.
580 did not cast votes. I expect it's likely a majority of those non-voters were probably more-broad-minded
guys who had legitimate and important business in the hallways or at a coffee table. But if 54 of those 580
had been in the room and voting for Cal (9% of the non-voters), he would have been elected.

And Ray Cannata was elected rather than Dominic Aquila by only 9 votes.
I suppose, to emphasize the point, we could say that in committee elections in Greenville, 56% of the
registered commissioners did not vote, and two elections were determined by very close votes, one of which
was just a 9 vote difference.

Though we did not initially have any crucial votes to consider I would like to add a vote for today.




The RPR report did not appear to have an item of significance until the following minority report was shared with me.
You’ll note that a perfectly reasonable exception is taken, in the best possible spirit, on the Sabbath. Yet this was
seen by some on the committee as an exception. That may sound minuscule, but when the RPR report is given
(1.30pm today), if this minority report is passed due in part to our absence, it will only add to the spirit of hostility
fostered between candidates, their presbyteries, and the committee that reviews their minutes. If we believe we are a
denomination where this sort of charitable latitude on the Sabbath is given, we must soundly reject this minority
report. Sorry to pull you all in for just this one vote, but if we’re trying to change the way the Assembly functions, and
the committees function, we have to react to this sort of overreach when it happens. Accepting the minority report
tightens the screws on Sabbath views without justification.




The report is slated for 1.30, and this is in the second half of the report, the minutes for Philadelphia Metro
West. Best estimate is 2pm, but we will let you know if it seems to be much later or any earlier.

Brothers,

There is one item for the Administrative committee that bears full engagement: Recommendation 3 to form a study
committee to study women’s roles in the church.




Today, from the Administrative Committee, we will be voting on a recommendation to form a study committee to study
the issue of woman serving in the ministries of the church. Wherever you come down in your own thoughts on these
issues, please keep in mind that the study committee advocated by the CMC and the Administrative Committee comes to
the floor of the General Assembly as a unanimous recommendation. Some might ask: why then did the Admin CoC reject
the motion overwhelmingly by a vote of 31-7? You would have to ask the majority in the committee, but many
arguments formed around fear of losing our orthodoxy, much like the PCUSA. But this strikes me as an unfounded fear.
More to the point, the remedy is worse than the disease: to restrain the liberty of our denomination to study an issue for
the sake of orthodoxy is to protect nothing at all. When we fear the opening of God’s word, and the studying of it by fully
ordained men, for fear of unorthodoxy, we deprive one another of the very means of Orthodoxy. We starve at the dinner
table. We would urge you to vote for further study.




I’ve asked TE Peter Rowan to write a quick summary of our position:

Study committees along these lines have been voted down in previous assemblies, yet this issue has continued to
resurface and will continue to do so until we are willing to enter in to this conversation and consider this for our time and
for our people. . This unanimity speaks loudly to this need. Our denomination has done a great job at telling women
what they are not allowed to do, what is desired most in this kind of study committee is to ask what they can and should
do. You may find the nature of the committee too broad, you may find a particular issue to be one that you find
completely settled, but please consider that the Spirit is with our church and will guide this committee in its work. We
can - and we will - debate the particularities of what the committee is charged with, but please consider voting in favor of
this committee in belief that the Spirit has been present with the study committee of the CMC, the CMC and the
Administrative Committee.

This year we believe the Nominating Committee has given us a fine slate of candidates. In each case we are
supportive of the committee. The following is a list of the candidate we would support along with floor
nominations submitted during this GA to challenge the committee nominees. There are several reasons for
recommending a candidate, as we are exhorted in the committee meeting. Special preference is given to
geographical representation (some presbyteries and regions are oversampled) as well as a desire to allow for
a broader representation of age and experience on our committees. Again, in each case we are supportive of
the committee recommendation.

you know to do the same. He has served previously; it would be a grievous error to allow him to serve again.

While we wait for the Administrative Committee Report, I’m sharing our advice on Overtures compiled from
men on the committee. The basic advice is below, followed by specific perspectives on overtures:



Additional thoughts on specific overtures:

Regarding Overture 14

I have been thinking of more lines of thought on 14

On 14, it seems to me as we debated in overtures that some are afraid of being able to deal adequately with
heretics without this added power (and be clear this is giving an added power to the prosecutor.) But it should
be pointed out that the most controversial recent case, that involving Peter Leithart, would not have been
helped by this change. Leithart did testify on his own behalf. So while it seems that some are concerned that
we need this additional power in order to deal with false teachers this is just not the case. If you need an officer
to testify against himself, if you have to compel him to do so, then I would argue you need to build a stronger
case.

On the other hand, there are many who are afraid of the unknown, of the tool that this might give an
overzealous prosecutor, who perhaps hears through the grapevine or the blogesphere about something a pastor
said and wants to go after the guy. This change, this added power, could be used to go after a guy where there
is insufficient other evidence, and I would submit to you that this is a very real danger.

Thus I urge you to vote down this change which will add nothing necessary for a legitimate case with ample
evidence, but open the door for nuisance cases and overzealous prosecutions.



Regarding Overture 39

From the RE from Nashville who was on overtures, Bryce Sullivan. He gave me permission to share and
include his name.

I thought these issues in 39 were confusing and, of course, arcane, and I didn't study this carefully before GA.
But here's what I think.

First, I now disagree with replacing "complaint" with "appeal" on line 16. It seems to me that a complaint is
made against an action of the court, so the term complaint seems to work. The outcome of discipline without
process is an action of a court and not the result of a trial. An appeal is only used in a very specific case, and I
don’t think discipline without process is one of these.

This is a primary question. Is the discipline without process a trial or simply "an act of the court?" Of course,
they are modifying 42-2 as well, but this change seems wrong to me. 42-2 no says: Only those who have
submitted to a regular trial are entitled to an appeal.

Looking at 38-1, you see that the discipline without process is done before the court, but no mention is made
of a trial. The fact that it is “without process” indicates there is no trial. So in order for this amendment to be
made, 42-2 is also being amended--but is that enough?

38-1. When any person shall come forward and make his offense known to the court, a full statement of the
facts shall be recorded and judgment rendered without process. In handling a confession of guilt, it is
essential that the person intends to confess and permit the court to render judgment without process.
Statements made by him in the presence of the court must not be taken as a basis of a judgment without
process except by his consent. In the event a confession is intended, a full statement of the facts should be
approved by the accused, and by the court, before the court proceeds to a judgment. The accused has the
right of complaint against the judgment.

I think in the case a discipline without process, the man must submit to the court's action. The appeal would
be by way of complaint against the court's action. I don't think the same protections need to be in place for
discipline without process when compared to a trial.
Jedidiah Slaboda
<jedidiahslaboda@gmail.com>

Brothers,
It's that time again. There are a number of things we need to be doing to prepare for General Assembly:
      1. Participating in committee work
      2. Volunteering for a General Assembly Committee of Commissioners
The primary issues related to this assembly revolve around last year's action of the Cooperative Ministries
Committee (CMC), a committee composed - among others - of the heads of each permanent committee and the
last five moderators of the General Assembly. Last year the CMC, in keeping with it's charter to propose any
changes beneficial to furthering the work of the church, proposed a study committee on the role of women in
ministry. We expect the committee to bring a final report this year. Due heavy conflict surrounding the
recommendation of the CMC, some believe that the committee overstepped by proposing a study committee
through the Administrative committee instead of presbytery overture, we anticipate a number of overtures
seeking to restrict the work of the CMC. There will also be overtures seeking to block the reception of the
report from the women in ministry study committee. Expect a few overtures that seek to preemptively limit the
"damage" of the report by "clarifying" BCO language (no women deacons, etc.).


1. News
-Six overtures have officially been received by the Stated Clerk. Any necessary discussions will take place a
little closer to the assembly - probably in May - on the NP website.
-Overture 2 from Calvary seeks to give BCO 59 full constitutional status, the aim to make statements like,
"marriage is between one man and one woman" more clearly a part of our constitution, and driven by concerns
over PCA ministers eventually being compelled to officiate same-sex marriages. http://www.pcaac.org/wp-
content/uploads/2012/10/Overture-2-Calvary-BCO-59-Marriage.pdf
-Overture 3 from Westminster seeks to dismiss, with apologies, the women in ministry study committee. The
Assembly would be apologizing for their previous action. http://www.pcaac.org/wp-
content/uploads/2016/12/Overture-3-Westminster-Role-of-Women.pdf
-Overture 4 from Northwest Georgia seeks to specify that the role of Elder or Deacon is not open to
women. http://www.pcaac.org/wp-content/uploads/2012/10/Overture-4-Northwest-Georgia-Add-BCO-24-
11.pdf
-Overture 5 from Rocky Mountain seeks to further elaborate on the role of Elder. Perhaps some helpful
additions to a section that is far too thin. http://www.pcaac.org/wp-content/uploads/2012/10/Overture-5-
Rocky-Mtn-BCO-8-1-8-3.pdf
-Overture 6 from Gulfstream is another attempt to strengthen constitutional documents against same-sex
marriage views and practices. This one wants to see additional proof texts in our confession regarding
marriage. http://www.pcaac.org/wp-content/uploads/2012/10/Overture-6-Gulfstream-WCF-add-proof-texts.pdf



2. Action items:
First: Sign up for Nominating Committee OR Review of Presbytery Records
PERMANENT COMMITTEES:
We’ve mentioned the value of participation in the Review of Presbytery Records committee and the
Nominating Committee as two key ways you can do your part to preserve the health of our denomination.
Participation in the NP means, at a minimum, that you will be involved in helping to fill committee spots from
your presbytery. Either volunteer yourself for open slots on RPR and the Nominating Committee (The NC
recommends members for the permanent committees and the SJC to the floor of the General Assembly) or find
a friend in your presbytery to volunteer. Your Winter meeting is the likely point for nominations to these
committees. The Nominating Committee is a one-day meeting in Atlanta, Saturday March 25th. Your travel
and accommodation (you will likely travel Friday night for this meeting) is reimbursed by the denomination.
Review of Presbytery Records meets for a few days during one week in May, your travel and accommodation
is reimbursed for this meeting.

Review of Presbytery Records: Serving on RPR is a three-day commitment May 31-June 2 - and the PCA
pays the expenses. This committee reviews the minutes presbyteries submit to the General Assembly to ensure
the minutes of presbytery are in order – that is, there are no strange deviations, exceptions recorded that strike
at the vitals of our system, etc. What sounds perhaps mild-mannered has become a battleground for theological
axe-grinding. While there are many who seek to serve on this committee for less litigious reasons, there are
others who see RPR as an opportunity to move the lines of orthodoxy in the PCA. It is always necessary to
have wise and qualified men serving in this committee to ensure that our denomination is accurately
represented in these discussions.

Committee of Commissioners. This is the committee work done on site at the General Assembly
TE Joel St. Clair put together a helpful priority list for committee work:

TIER 1 - Playing with the New England Patriots of the polity world
- Overtures (TE)
- Overtures (RE)
- MTW
- Admin
- Interchurch Relations

TIER 2 - Rookie league (or grizzly veterans who need to rest their hurting/broken bodies) from a polity
perspective
- CTS
- MNA
- CDM
- RUM

TIER 3 - These are where they hand out participation medals to everyone serving;
- Foundation
- CC
- RBI
- Ridgehaven

-Contact your presbytery's clerk and simply ask to be assigned to a committee. Your process may involve a
nominating committee for your presbytery or they may appoint anyone willing. Don't be afraid to be bold; we
all have a right to do the work of the broader church, even if the same people seem to serve every year. speak
up.


-You can register for the Overtures committee up to the day it meets, the Monday of the assembly. Would you
consider filling an empty spot in your presbytery? The committee debate will be sharp and particularly
important this year.



3. Calendar:
June 12-16: The Assembly meets in Greensboro, NC (which is in the south, incredibly)

Brothers,

It's time to prep for General Assembly. If you've wondered why you haven't received many partnership emails
it is partly due to the fact that I'm trying not to wear us all out before we hit Greensboro. So let's dig in.
1. News

"marriage is between one man and one woman" more clearly a part of our constitution, and driven by concerns
over PCA ministers eventually being compelled to officiate same-sex marriages. http://www.pcaac.org/wp-

OVERTURES SEEKING TO CHANGE THE ROLE OF THE CMC

In some of the speeches from the floor opposing the formation of the Women in Ministry Study Committee,
there was doubt cast on the proper role of the Cooperative Ministries Committee and whether it should be able
to present business before a committee of the Assembly (in this case, the Administrative Committee). My own
view is that the CMC cannot plan without the ability to move business; I'm sure we've all experienced
committees that are all rudder and no sail. These overtures have in common a desire to shrink the profile of the
CMC in regards to Assembly business.



-Overture 7 from Northern New England seeks to "remove long-range planning" from the CMC

2. Action items:

-Please let us know if you are on a Committee of Commissioners for GA. I will post those group memberships
so you can connect if you have a question about something going on in a particular committee.

-READ THE WIMSC REPORT. It's important to form your own conclusions about the findings of the study
committee before engaging in discussion. This will be the primary topic of discussion during the week. Here's
a link to the report: http://www.pcaac.org/wp-content/uploads/2017/04/Women-Serving-in-Ministry-of-
Church-Ad-Interim-Report-2017.pdf

Irwyn Ince, NP member and chairman of the committee wrote a helpful brief intro to the work of the
committee and I'm sharing it here with his permission:


"Brothers, let me share some foundational (and other) thoughts with you about our committee's makeup, goals, and
work. I hope that what I write here is helpful, provides clarity, and encourages you that we've taken a healthy step
forward. Much of what I'm about to say I'll repeat in the seminar at GA, but there are other things that I'm only
going to say here.
Our Makeup. George Robertson did an excellent job in selecting the committee members (I only question his
selection of convener!). With regard to special committees appointed to investigate a question, Robert's Rules says it
is important that all the different views of the membership be reflected in the committee members. This is what
George did. He wrote to me earlier this week and said by far this is the most difficult and potentially incendiary task
that our Church has taken up in the three decades he's served the PCA. So, although the members' views may differ,
he wanted people who would put the peace and purity of the Church before themselves. Also note the point made in
lines 30-38 on page 2404 of the report. He fought for the establishment of this study committee and was thankfully
sustained.
Our Goals. Given the diversity of views on our committee I made a strategic decision at the outset. I can rightly be
criticized on this point, so feel free to do so. I made it clear to the committee that we were going to pursue a
consensus report. This meant that everyone was going to have to give on certain points. We could have easily
pressed into two camps on the committee. That approach would've inevitably lead to a majority and a minority
report. What would follow from this is a blood bath on the floor of GA over the issue of women in the diaconate,
and a significant potential to lose the middle over the broader issue of women in the ministry of the church. I was
determined to pursue a course that would move the conversation on that issue forward without making this GA all
about women deacons. That is only a PART of our non-health as a denomination when it comes to women. The
report has holes for sure, but I believe it is clear throughout that we are calling our church to stop the detrimental
way of talking about women in terms of what they cannot do in the church, and to joyfully pursue how women are
called to use their gifts for the blessing of the church. This calling is well beyond our overly patriarchal (and often
mysogynistic) approach to ministry.
Our Work. Another determination that I and the committee made was that we would report in one year and not
request an extension. This was a strategic decision that came with understanding that our report would not be all that
it could be. We would be opening ourselves to even more criticism because there are plenty of people who have
personally or corporately studied this issue and will point to things we didn't consider or left out. At the same time, I
do stand by our work as a whole. When it comes to the animating (in this group and elsewhere) recommendations
that come from our work, understand that the committee's view is that we are taking a step forward. They're not
intended to be an end to dialogue, but a way of engaging the start of dialogue. Please note that it says "select and
appoint godly women of the congregation to assist the ORDAINED leadership." This isn't just about deacons. We
are calling for sessions and presbyteries to appoint women to assist the elders as well. We are saying that we need
godly women to assist the session in its work, and that our churches need to be intentional in identifying and making
room for women who are gifted in shepherding."



3. Calendar:

June 12-16: The Assembly meets in Greensboro, NC (which is in the south, incredibly)




More next week.
