to thenp@googlegroups.com


Today there are a few goals I would recommend for the group:
   1. Pass the unordained persons serving on committees overture through the OC
   2. Elect Howie Donahoe for moderator
   3. Defeat last year’s BCO 30-1 (see explanation below)
   4. Defeat the Interchurch Relations CoC recommended withdrawal from the NAE. (See
       explanation below)
   5. Maintain your charity, hope, and grit.

HOWIE DONAHOE
I want to take a moment to share why I will be voting for Howie instead of John Bise. This is not a
vote against John Bise. He has served in many capacities in the Lord’s Church and the PCA in
particular. He is kind and willing to help wherever needed. This is more about the opportunity to elect
someone who will serve not only as the moderator of a meeting but for the next five years as an
advisor on the Cooperative Ministries Committee, a position that requires us to look beyond good
sentiment in favor of John and toward the gifts we need to utilize in the PCA for the current cultural
moment.
Howie has served as an elder for 32 years. In four churches across three presbyteries - sea to
shining sea. He has served on every level of denominational leadership at the Assembly, Presbytery,
and local church. He is currently serving multiple terms on the SJC, helping us sort through cases
like those related to our SSA debates. This is not to mention his collection of whiskeys and his
willingness to kindly share them. But it isn’t just his service to the PCA that compels me.
Howie left a church where he was connected and beloved to go to another PCA church in his area
that needed an extra elder to help them through a transition. He has been willing to personally travel
across the country to encourage struggling ministers. I love that view of Eldership in the local church.
The fact is that John is not what some call “political,” something many of us have heard by those
preferring Bise for moderator. What that means is that we don’t know his views on the role of the
church or a vision for the PCA in general. We know Howie’s views, his help to many of us in
understanding the polity of the PCA, the countless hours he spends making sure that we are thinking
through the long-term impact of all those RAO and BCO amendments. We know his care for and
dedication to the PCA and the ministry of the local church. And as we are trying to think more
carefully and deeply about how our denomination will speak and minister in a culture of mistrust and
fear, I want as many voices like Howie’s in the room as possible. We are looking not only for
someone whose service to the church we can celebrate, but whose leadership we can follow. Howie
is that guy.

LAST YEAR’S BCO 30-1
At the beginning of Assembly business we deal with amendments that passed last year’s assembly,
and voting by presbyteries, and now must pass a second assembly. Part of the reason for that long
process is that in the intervening year we may see issues with an overture that we missed initially.
Last year’s BCO 30-1 is an example of just this kind of overture. I reviewed Bryan Chapell’s advice
on this and I agree with him that we need to vote this down. I am including the text of a speech you
will hear later on the issue. I would recommend you be ready to vote against this overture:

Item 3 (Amendment to BCO 30-1) attempts a commendable revision, but in doing so creates another problem that contradicts the
spirit of the revision. The proposed wording would formalize a common misunderstanding of the biblical rules of discipline.


That misunderstanding is that the progressive steps of discipline are to be exercised without exception or discretion –
automatically – whenever there is not satisfactory repentance. The key revisions proposed are not merely permissive (saying what
actions “may” be taken, as in the original opening sentence), nor are they procedural (saying what steps you “should” follow if
                                                                                     nd
you must progress), but rather the revisions include a directive “shall” (in the 2        and last sentences), saying that you
must progress to indefinite suspension or excommunication, unlessthere is satisfactory repentance. Such a mandate removes from
local sessions the discretion needed to shepherd with the discernment and conviction needed to evaluate what is best for the
spiritual health of individuals and the overall church.
Let’s be clear, Jesus, in Matthew 18, does describe the steps you must follow if you go to the end of the path of church
discipline; but, the Savior does not teach that you must always go to the end of the path. The procedure is
mandatory, progression to a predetermined end is not.
To contend that we must go to the end of the path for every known-, not-fully-repented sin is:
1) To require the excommunication of all who overeat and who do not reach proper weight;
2) To require the parent of every wayward teen to submit their child to church discipline; or for the church to discipline such
parents for the mistakes others believe they have made.
We are not so mechanical, not because we are compromisers, but because we are committed toexercising proper, pastoral and
sessional discretion about what is best for the overall health of church and her people in terms of the initiation, timing,
advancement and degree of discipline.
We remember that love covers a multitude of sins (1 Pet. 1:8); and, that “God’s patience waited in the days of Noah” (1 Pet 3:20). He
even “endured with much patience vessels of wrath prepared for destruction, in order to make known the riches of his glory for
vessels of mercy” (Rom 9:22–23).


Calvin, who strongly advocated the conscientious and non-prejudicial application of church discipline so that “the contagion of
sin would not invade the multitude,” nonetheless was remarkably balanced in how he advised the church to proceed with
“discrimination” and without “disruptive severity.” He advised, “… Great severity is not to be used in lighter sins, but verbal
chastisement is enough -- and that mild and fatherly -- which should not harden or confuse the
sinner….” Calvin commended Augustine’s advice against using church trials to discipline public drunkenness because he said it
would empty the churches. Some sins, he argued can be corrected by formal discipline, but others will require a generation of
sound preaching. [Inst. IV.12.1-13]
By all means, we should follow biblical procedures when advancing through the steps of discipline, but to
mandate constitutionally that every sin be moved to higher orders of discipline until there is full and satisfactory repentance will
make either tyrants or compromisers of us all.
Local sessions must be allowed discretionary decisions about when to commence and how far to take church discipline in order
to preserve the life and health of the church and her children. This is our shepherding responsibility that requires me to ask you to
vote against this well-intended but improperly-worded amendment. Send it back for more biblically-balanced wording.




PROPOSED WITHDRAWL FROM THE NAE
The permanent committee - Interchurch Relations - recommends against withdrawing
from the NAE. But a very stacked committee with some of the farthest, hardest right
voices in the PCA took over committee work yesterday and passed a resolution
withdrawing from the association. We knew this would happen as it has been the
stated goal by many who, curiously, are simultaneously seeking to withdraw the PCA
from the NAE while threatening, for many years, to leave the PCA themselves. I want
to remind us of the advice of Bruce O’Neil on this matter:
“This is similar to the effort to leave the NAE back in 2011. Obviously the NAE is a
broad Christian organization made up of denominations that we would not agree with
on many theological issues, but find broad agreement on many more. I am a member
of the Interchurch Relations Committee and think it would be unhelpful to the PCA
and the NAE if we leave. The PCA is typically looked to for biblical and theological
guidance within in NAE. We have a disproportionate influence for the size of our
denomination within NAE. Roy Taylor, our stated clerk, has served as the chairman
of the Board for NAE for more than a decade. But I also believe the PCA would be
impoverished by our leaving the NAE. The Mission of God is so much bigger than the
PCA and being part of NAE demonstrates we recognize this fact and are willing to
cooperate whenever we can. I hope that this will be defeated in the Committee of
Commissioners, but if not surely the Assembly will not support such a narrow view of
the mission. The IRC has adopted a lengthy response to this overture which will be
part of our report at GA.”
SCHEDULE (Bold, underlined, are the most critical moments for you to be on the floor!)
8a: Overtures committee continues
1.30p: Opening worship service (Irwyn Ince preaching!)
3p: MODERATOR ELECTION.
3.45p: IRC REPORT (this is the NAE withdrawl I recommend we defeat)

Finally I want to remind us of this: some of you may not have experienced GA week before, or at
least you haven’t been this involved before. We are thankful for you. There are many in the PCA who
have been bearing the weight of defending it against attacks from all sides, who have championed
things like Good Faith subscription, who have pushed forward racial reconciliation, who have had to
run back into the assembly hall for difficult votes, who have had to refocus and re-enlist their energy
after late-night debates and frustrating results. You are a breath of fresh air for them. You are a sign
that all of it is worth the work. Please do not be discouraged by early results, whatever they may be.
The week is long and the story of the church is much longer.
We are all, even our beloved opposition, united in a future that will renew all things long after the
last clicker malfunction prompts the last point of personal privilege. We are beloveds of the beloved
together. Beloved enemies sometimes, fighting like siblings do, and there will be some unfair things
said and unfair accusations. But let’s be men and women of grit. Let’s not be the first ones to forget
that we serve the great King, who loves us so. I know you know all of this, but sometimes good news
needs to be rehearsed. He loves us, and we serve him before we serve committees or churches, or
nefarious and clandestine secret PCA societies. Much love to you all, it’s time to get on the grind and
be counted this week.

Day 2 review:
I gave you a some goals for today’s assembly business:

    1. Pass the unordained persons serving on committees overture through the OC - This failed.
        But we discovered in the course of business that the Overtures Committee is far to the right
        of the Assembly. This gives me hope that we will be able to win this one the floor. We have a
        powerful minority report given by E J Nusbaum and some compelling speeches.
    2. Elect Donahoe for moderator: passed 62-38%. That is quite a margin. Of course Howie was
        very duly and deservedly elected but it might tell us something about what the floor’s
        composition will be. There was heavy electioneering for Bise, and yet his nomination failed
        by 22%. John is a fine man, I want to say that again. But I see this as a win for all the
        reasons given this morning.
    3. We defeated the 30-1 amendment from last year (and another related to legal representation
        in presbyteries) that I felt were not helpful. That’s a victory.
    4. Defeat recommended withdrawal from NAE. The assembly did not complete that business. It
        will be the first thing taken up at 9.30a.
So in general a good day.

Friends,

I would like to recommend some particular goals for our work today:



1. Defeat the overture to withdraw from the NAE: DONE

2. Defeat some particularly unhelpful nominations to permanent committees

3. Approve Overture 42 and answer all other SSA-related overtures to it

4. Approve Overture 8 regarding unordained persons on committees



Regarding nominations - I know it is difficult to vote against fellow brothers in Christ, so we should think
about it this way. Not every minister is gifted for every ministry. Our polity requires us to make those
judgments. Elder Mel Duncan’s lack of fit for the SJC and Wiley’s lack of fit for CTS seem beyond question
- though I am glad to hear your perspective on this - and as always please vote your conscience! The
recommendations for the Nominating committee report were covered in an earlier email this morning.



Regarding Overture 42: There will be a procedural motion offered that would allow us to save 2/3 of our
time on the docket while also presenting an opportunity to avoid speaking primarily through unhelpful
and non-pastoral overtures concerning SSA. Overture 42 also incorporates every other paper and study
(including the Nashville Statement and Missouri Presbytery study committee). Be aware this will be at
the beginning of the Overtures committee report. Be ready to vote.



Today’s the day. One day for all the business; one day that affects the work of our denomination for
years to come. Thankful for all of you, for speaking and for keeping your friends up to date, and for
correcting me when I’m off track. Much love to you, friends. Let’s get on the grind.

Potential Nashville Statement Talking Points

tag...@gmail.com

to The National Partnership

I'd try to say some of these things myself but I was on Overtures. If you're looking to speak up
tonight and you have the opportunity maybe this can help get you started:



POINT 1
Two sides of debate on all these Overtures regarding human sexuality.
The two sides focus on one of two audiences.
First Audience: the faithful Christian who does not struggle with non-straight sexual
desire (my grandma)
Second Audience: the believer or skeptic trying to understand their own non-straight
sexual desire
Most of our debate basically argues: ignore one audience and minister to the other.
Both sides have said things that sound like this to the other side.
We are talking past each other!


So think about who you are picturing in your mind – which audience are you
ministering to?
How would you change your words if you wanted the other side of the debate to be
able to hear you?


Please answer Overture 4 in the negative.


POINT 2
How can we declare the Nashville Statement as a “biblically faithful” statement,
which although it has biblical language in parts, it does not have one single Bible
reference?
Who are we seeking to talk to? To whom are we declaring things?
Without Biblical references, we must be seeking to talk to people who already know
the Bible.
However, the rhetoric around this statement indicates we are trying to stand up to
the culture.
This statement does not communicate clearly to the culture. It actually alienates and
confuses the culture.


Please answer Overture 4 in the negative.

“Well, that’s a genetic fallacy!” Not if the argument for supporting the NS is clarity. Our people have
the internet. What will we say when they ask us why we got something from the likes of CBMW?



2. Closely related, take off the CBMW name. There’s no way someone exercising intellectual
honesty would support something from that kinda organization when other options are available.



3. Theological laziness - Not doing a study committee looks intellectually lazy & we miss a huge
opportunity to make something official. And while we do a study committee pastors can refer their
people back to the WLC on the 6th commandment.



4. Pastoral tone deafness - Supporting this Statement will place unnecessary roadblocks for many
ministers in our denomination. It’s the equivalent of using a sledgehammer to remove wisdom teeth.




Jason Cornwell

Another common argument i’m hearing this week is, “people with SSA should not be treated as

unique sort of way, then why are we talking so much about their sin and not other sins more hetero
in nature, or for that matter gossip, slander, gluttony, drinking too much, and loveless marriages, all
of which are much more ubiquitous in our tribe then same sex kind of stuff?

47th GA Reflection
11 views

                                                 



james
<james@newcityhilliard.org>

Jun 28, 2019, 11:39:13 AM

                                                 
                                                 
                                                 
to thenp@googlegroups.com

Dear friends,

The 47th GA is in the books. If you remember I listed three of my personal goals for this assembly:

1. Reject Nashville statement and approve SSA study committee

2. Approve the Abuse study committee

3. Approve Overture 8 regarding the service of unordained people on committees
1.5/3. Not what I was hoping for, by any means. Please allow me to share my personal thoughts; this
is not what I think you ought to think, but what I think about all of this.



1. The Nashville statement felt like a bigger blow than I prepared for. After spending time talking to
people and hearing their stories of struggle, watching the debate on the floor, the importance of
rejecting the statement grew in my estimation. It wasn’t important because the statement will have a
long life in the PCA - I don’t think it will - but because it was such a painful reminder of the chasm
that exists between two sides in our denomination regarding what some call cultural engagement but
others, me included, would call beautiful orthodoxy. The debate on this overture happened right after
an incredible vision of the Kingdom in worship. That’s the way it is, of course, rejoicing and grieving
in stunning proximity until Jesus returns and all we’ve lost in grief comes washing back in, like the
tide.



2. I am optimistic about the work of a study committee that can live in the PCA for at least the next
generation as an expression of the very best of the thinking our God has given us at this time and in
this place. I’m confident we will hear from people that will challenge us to drink deeply from truth, not
truth like grape juice, but long-suffering, complex, aged wine - a nice port!, as it is embodied in our
suffering savior. That’s something to look forward to, and something for our prayers. The study
committee would have been a stiff fight, I’m convinced, if we had not so fiercely signaled our
commitment on the floor of the committee and the assembly. It was clear to the opposition that this
was going to happen. That was a well-fought outcome and a critical “win.”



3. I asked someone who actually knows something about RPR to speak to what was another
positive outcome this week:

Review of Presbytery Records

RPR continues to be a source of health for our denomination. This is largely due to the face-to-face
interaction that many of the commissioners have on the porch after hours at the hotel. Beverages
are shared. Cigars and pipes are smoked. Men share about their families and ministries. It is not
uncommon to see two men who have had passionate debate earlier in the day praying for each
other later that night. RPR Chairman Ken McHeard was not lying about the RPR being the best
committee in the PCA. It is hard, detailed work. But men have grown to trust each other over the
years. Men who may disagree on some significant issues. But nonetheless they know each other
and trust that the person looking from across the aisle is a good man, a man who loves Jesus and
his church.

This year men came together from across the aisle to call a presbytery to account for mishandling a
case involving spousal abuse. This is the third year in a row that the RPR has held firm in holding
presbyteries to account on this issue. This is one of the reasons we are having a study committee
on domestic abuse this year - because the RPR kept the issue on the front burner.

Another issue that has been kept on the front burner by the RPR is the issue of holding fast to good
faith subscription. This year, for the second year in a row, the General Assembly has agreed with
RPR's recommendation on Calvary Presbytery that they may not forbid a man to teach his views on
a stated difference. This year, two other instances were joined from NW Georgia and Ohio Valley
and the GA likewise concurred. For two years in a row, the GA has spoken to the issue of upholding
good faith subscription.


The reason why we kept pushing the issue at RPR is the same reason why we kept the abuse issue
on the front burner: a larger change is needed. Several men came to the mics on the floor of GA and
expressed that the way to handle this issue was to make a change to the Book of Church Order.
Such a change is currently in the works. This is why we keep issues on the front burner at RPR, and
the end game is lasting change through amendment of our constitution




4. The overture to allow the unordained to serve on boards and committees continues to work its
way forward. It may not seem that way in a year it was defeated by a large margin, but consider: a.
We learned more about the barriers we must overcome to see unordained men and women laboring
fully in the denomination, b. we gained valuable allies, men and women, through the process of
debate who I believe will give us critical tools for success, and c. An unassailable minority report -
authored by EJ Nusbaum and Bruce Terrell, is now a strong line of argument to which the other side
will have to answer. Wonderful work was done this year - I am only more emboldened and assured
that this is the trajectory of the PCA, a more orthodox view of ordained authority and the priesthood
of all believers. Next year we will knock again, at the door, and see what our Lord does.




5. We were able to defeat what I think were not helpful attempts to carve a cultural rather than
kingdom-minded mission for both MTW and Covenant Seminary. Attempts to drive back MTW’s
endorsement of non-ordained persons operating in non-ecclesiastical areas of expertise - were met
with very clear denial. And shots aimed at potentially severing our relationship with Covenant
Seminary were answered, in my opinion, with prejudice. This is a sign of health that has not always
existed in the PCA. Often some odd thing would be approved that would unduly embarrass a
committee chairman or institution and we would have to come back the next year and undo it. This
year that was stopped, immediately. Evidence of your influence in safeguarding the assembly, and a
real encouragement to both Dr. Dalbey and Lloyd Kim, who have led with humble and skilled
dedication to their charges.




6. Worship. Wow. What an incredible job done by the committee, guys like David Ridenhour and
Joshua Anderson. I was greatly moved by the beauty of the Word given to us through music and
prayer, incredible preaching, the sacrament, a blind woman who sang us all to sight, David Cassidy
goin’ y’aaaaaaaalll on us, Dr. Ince using his hanky because of all the fire. My goodness. A feast. How
about that?




7. The Gathering, was another reminder of what’s next. Lance LEWIS. Wonderful reminder that the
Gospel compels generosity without exception. The venue was great, the connections we could see
from agencies and parachurch and friend networks, showed us how big even this tiny church can be.
Also those bacon skewers were 100%.
8. Some things I found myself saying during the week:




- No one can stop us from becoming this kind of denomination. The denomination we were at the
end of worship last night. No one. We are the majority, and if we gather around the Gospel there will
be nothing to stop it. If we keep preaching to the dry bones.




- There’s work to be done, and it is clear that we are going to do it. You all were so invested in the
courts, trying to understand everything we were doing, proposing speeches, answering the bell
with passion. People still honestly think the NP is led by the hand but I no more lead than the bull
rider leads the bull. Hang on for dear life. You guys were a joy to watch in action.




Look, there’s much to be done, and it will be done. Thank God we get to go home to our churches,
where the real treasure is kept. Take a break from thinking about GA polity. We’ll connect again in
the beginning of the fall and talk about next steps. For now I am grateful to have seen how our Lord
is using you all for his glory. May he lead us to be good stewards in the PCA of the Gospel God has
given to the world.




For the King,

JK

NP NOTE: 2020.
4 views



james
<james@newcityhilliard.org>
Jan 9, 2020, 3:19:06 PM
                                                 
                                                 
                                                 
to thenp@googlegroups.com

{This communication is confidential and may not be shared without the author’s permission}


Friends,
As the first presbytery meetings of the year are upon us I wanted to give a bit of an overview for the
next six months of work and to share one big concern.

The big concern is this: while the last years of GA have provided some encouraging signs, a
particular tactical response from the unhealthy wing of the denomination has led to the packing of
committees by the minority for one reason: the majority has not been willing to speak up. In many
presbyteries, administrative committees have begun nominating candidates then bringing them to
the floor without a contested vote. Typically I hear surprise that a certain presbytery nominated one
of their most conservative members for the OC, for instance. For the past few years presbyteries
have moved nominations to Admin committees staffed with the more polity-minded and conservative
members. Those committees pass along a nominee that is less representative of the presbytery as a
whole and those of us who do not want to appear to be negative about a particular candidate, allow
that candidate to represent them, no contest.

We cannot continue to do this. While it may appear to be gracious to not contest a nominee serving
as your presbytery’s representative, it is not ungracious to ensure that the will of the presbytery is
clear. After all, we vote every year on the moderator for the assembly, for instance, without being
ungracious. It is not contentious to make sure that your presbytery sends representatives that reflect
the will of your presbytery, rather than a rep that is less broad-minded. The subtle inches we lose by
failing to pay attention when we are electing representatives can cost us yards later. So my
recommendation would be that you:

    1. Find out how your presbytery elects nominees for GA committees.
    2. If they’re not nominated by committee, but first-come-first-served, get your name in there -
       even now in January (big areas of need are always the Overtures Committee and the
       Administrative committee - particularly this year)
    3. IF they’re nominated by committees, ensure that those committee choices receive a vote
       on the floor.
    4. Make sure that you and others are present when those votes happen.
    5. If there are no suitable nominees on the slate, nominate someone else.
    6. Be willing to speak from the floor of your presbytery. It is possible to speak for a different
       candidate while not speaking against anyone.
One other thing: We have to show up at the assembly.

Most votes are 60/40, but that 60/40 can shift dramatically. When the majority isn’t particularly eager
to be on the floor for a vote, we leave the results in doubt. A majority committed to showing up at the
assembly can do great things. We will never work our connectionalism into something beautiful until
we show up regularly, together, to do the work of the assembly. We will have to be willing to bring
our Elders with us and participate.

At this year’s Assembly we will have a chance to elect a new Stated Clerk. We will be presented
with a candidate from the Administrative Permanent Committee. There may also be one or more
floor nominations presented by various factions. My read is that this could be an opportunity to move
our denomination toward clarifying our mission in the world and leveraging the incredible gifts we’ve
been given in the PCA to advance the work of the Kingdom. Roy Taylor has been a real blessing,
and his steady stewardship will be a significant challenge for the next one to hold his office. But the
right Stated Clerk could do more than keep the car on the road; he could help us move toward a
much healthier destination. The wrong clerk may or may not be a critical error, but the right one
could help us build a uniquely effective and generous and fruitful denomination. It’s worth the time
and expense to help make that happen this year. I hope you’ll join us.

If you’re new to the NP, expect another note as overtures begin to be received by the clerk’s office.
That’s when we will begin to know what the big issues will be, and how we can participate in the
process this year. Anticipate some rumblings in the usual places about human sexuality overtures,
Side-B/Revoice, etc. The study committee on human sexuality is expected to report at this GA.

In February and March we will focus on Nominating Committee, then the committees of the
Assembly itself. As always, we intend for the NP to serve as a forum for resourcing and
encouragement. While I and others will share opinions we want to be shaped by the wise dissent, if
you’ve got it. Unity, not uniformity.

Grateful to serve alongside all of you.

James Kessler

NP Note: Reviewing the AIC report, and moving forward.
13 views



james
<james@newcityhilliard.org>
Aug 20, 2020, 8:18:09 AM
                                                   
                                                   
                                                   
to thenp@googlegroups.com

[Please do not share without the permission of the author]


Dear friends,
August has always felt like the beginning of preparation for the next year’s GA. With the
postponement of this last assembly, the need to refocus and prepare is even more timely. Over the
next few weeks I’d like to propose some areas of focus for the health of our little corner of the
church. Today, though, let's reflect on the work of the Ad-Interim Committee as it examined SSA and
human sexuality. Understanding this report will help us navigate the next year’s overtures that will
seek to act contrary to its wisdom. My apologies for the length of this note - I tried to be concise
where I could.

Reviewing the AIC report:
After the General Assembly’s affirmation of the Nashville Statement as “Biblically faithful,” an action
many of us did not support, there was some hope that the PCA's own scholars, given the directive to
study the issue of a Biblical approach to same-sex attraction and human sexuality, might be able to
produce a document that could serve the denomination and the broader church to a far greater
degree. In certain corners of our denomination there was a significant skepticism about the
usefulness of the committee and some openly denigrated the work as hopelessly compromised by
liberal voices even before it began. The stakes for the PCA’s reputation outside the denomination
were significant, but to a degree the stakes inside the body were even higher. Would it be possible,
given the cultural moment and the disintegration of civil and charitable discourse, for our
denomination, through a study committee, to do theology faithfully, confessionally, publicly?

My view is that they met that high standard. The report is not only comprehensive, but strikingly
circumspect, respectfully and charitably so. It takes hard work and patience to examine all the sides
of an issue and to render judgments with love - I think they accomplished just that. In the 60+ pages
of the report I found not one attribution of sinful motive. (Think of how hard it is for us to get through
Twitter’s 240 characters in a discussion about human sexuality without impugning motives). From
that perspective the AIC’s report is not only a work of substance about its subject, it’s also instructive
for how we might conduct ourselves as a connectional church.
Since my reflections are full of strengths, I’ll begin with the only significant weakness I noted (there
will always be smaller disagreements about terminology et al.): it relates to the discussion on the
“Three Challenges for Christians Today” #1 (pp.35-36). Addressing the modern identity narrative -
this was not a strong understanding of the roots of identity ideology (the report roots it in a
freedom/power dialectic it calls Marxist and Nietzschean, which I think was a complete miss).
Engaging in our culture from this perspective will miss the heart of our hearers.

In terms of strengths I found many.
     1. The apologetics content was accessible and compelling. I found pages 34-44, an
         apologetic approach to human sexuality, to be critical reading. If there’s one section you
         should closely follow and notate, it’s that one. The annotated bibliography gives a great list of
         authors and readings with which to interact. I could imagine those sections (apologetics and
         bibliography) would be immediately helpful in pastoral care, not only of those struggling with
         SSA, but those in our churches who are anxious and fearful culture warriors on either side of
         this discussion. Reading this section of the report dissolved some of my apprehensions
         about pastoring my own church toward a Christian sexual ethic. Challenges two and three
         from the “Three Challenges” (pp.37-40), included this paragraph, relating to the way a
         Christian sexual ethic blessed antiquity, and was particularly strong:
"There was an immediate concrete result that all could see. By breaking the connection of sex with
the social order, Christianity guarded the vulnerable from exploitation. No man could demand sex of
a woman without giving up his independence and committing his whole life to her. No man could
demand sex from his servants. The vulnerable—women, slaves, and children—were protected by
the insistence that sex occur only within the safety of the covenantal union of marriage. But beyond
these practical results, the “underlying logic” of Christianity regarding sex went much further and
higher. It re-imagined sex as no longer a mere appetite that we could barely control but rather as a
joyous, even sacred, expression that reflects the way God is saving the world.” (p.38)

    2. The report engages the reader with pastoral intent. Even the brief statements of
        orthodoxy were framed as wholly Biblical and Confessional ethics rather than propositions.
        This was not a set of affirmations and denials, thankfully. The committee showed obvious
        concern for the human and kingdom impact of their truthfulness. This is how we must speak
        prophetically, with love. The AIC report was also not willing to provide statements of
        orthodoxy without a framework for human flourishing. In a number of places the authors
        share a concern for bald truth-telling, throwing propositional grenades. Including statements
        #11 (friendship) and 12 (repentance and hope) in the report’s Twelve Statements were clear
        signs of Pastoral Theology at work. I was thankful for their thoroughness in helping pastors
        to pastor.
Regarding shepherding of the faithful, but weary, repenter: “We would never say to a new believer
who has a history of destructive anger, ‘Now that you are a Christian, you will never again feel a
rush of anger rise up within you at the wrong time, for a selfish reason, out of proportion to the
situation, or in any other way that contradicts God’s law.” Neither should we communicate to a
believer with a history of homosexual attraction the expectation that this will simply
disappear.’” (II.B.2 “Continued Corruption,” p.22)


There was even confessional hope for our imperfect obedience to Christ: "It is crucially important
that our churches communicate to same-sex attracted believers experiencing same-sex
attraction that faithfulness to God’s call to discipleship upon their lives is possible. An unclear
understanding of the Reformed position that sinful temptations themselves, as well as sins of the
will, are to be repented of might reasonably lead some to believe that faithfulness is impossible and
pursuing holiness is an exercise in futility. We should be clear that while every Christian’s obedience
remains imperfect and tainted by sin in this life, there is still a very real and important sense in which
through Christ all Christians have been equipped for real and progressive obedience to God that
brings him honor and is worthy of rejoicing in (WCF 16.6). This remains true even if their attraction to
the same sex does not go away.” (p.24)

On the Church’s responsibility to function as the invitational, welcoming, feasting community of the
King: “We ought to grieve any time a person who experiences attraction toward the same sex finds a
greater welcome and belonging in the secular LGBT community instead of the church.” (p.31)

    3. The report was willing to draw healthy lines regarding current controversies. The first
       third of the report deals comprehensively with questions of desire and temptation as well as
       what it means to “mortify the flesh.” All helpful. Of course this is the statement that will
       generate the most conflict from some corners of our denomination and broader
       evangelicalism: "For those repentant believers who know the struggle of same-sex
       attraction, our churches may welcome them not merely as broken people to be ministered to,
       but also as active and important participants and contributors in our communities. Like all
       yet-to-be- glorified Christians, those who struggle with same-sex attraction are commanded
       to walk with the Lord in faith and repentance. Insofar as such persons display the requisite
       Christian maturity, we do not consider this sin struggle automatically to disqualify someone
       for leadership in the church (1 Cor. 6:9-11, 1 Tim. 3:1-7, Titus 1:6-9; 2 Pet. 1:3-11).” (p.31)
The report provided wisdom, pastoral care, and clear guidance. Praise God for His care for us
through His word.




A word about our present and future. I think this report gives us a glimpse into the present and
future of the PCA. I’m aware, as I’m sure many of you are, of the various micro-groups whose vision
is wrapped around existential threats to the survival of Biblical fidelity in our denomination. Some of
those groups once sought to win votes, but having failed to do so, are attempting to cultivate division
within individual churches by urging congregants to confront their pastors in a posture of distrust. I
understand it is troubling to have a kind-hearted congregant ask you if you are now or ever have
been a member of the National Partnership, as if it is some dishonor to your office. It is disturbing to
have congregants bring up particular pastors in other corners of the denomination, friends of yours
who you know to be exemplary leaders and shepherds, and to have their name mentioned as proof
of theological liberalism, because they saw it on social media, or on some website. It is indeed hard
to hold to a beautiful vision for our calling when it isn’t the sheep who cause you the most distress
but the shepherds of the sheep. When pastors defame pastors it cuts us all off at the knees. It would
be tempting to buy into either a hyper localism (I’ll just keep my eyes fixed on my local church) or
hyper denominationalism (our hope is in eliminating the influence of bad actors). I want to encourage
you, as I encourage myself, to adopt the method of cultural engagement, care for the sheep, and the
gentle answer of our critics, embodied in the AIC’s report. If a congregant wants to know about the
National Partnership, feel free to tell them what it is. If they trust their pastor, it will be a good
opportunity to share the burden you feel for what we can accomplish as a connectional church, and
why you must do the hard work of being a churchman. If they don’t trust you, the issues are far
deeper than being a part of an email list for the good of the Church. What I tell my own people is
simple: I’m better when I reason together with others. I don’t know everything. I need help to fulfill my
duty as a presbyter.

There are a few implications for the NP:
   1. I continue to see the need for our little collective to exist - navigating controversies and
       micro-divisions while leveraging the gifts of so many of you for the greater good of the
       denomination (e.g. have you read Todd Gwennap’s article about 2019’s protest? Really good
       - https://www.toddgwennap.com/blog/2020/8/7/why-i-signed-the-pca-protest) will
       require clear-headed collaboration. It’s only going to get harder to lead with wisdom. We
       need each other’s help.



   2. Rather than focus on NP growth, it is my hope that we serve as instigators of good in the
      PCA. The Beautiful Orthodoxy conference is an example of this, not a Partnership
      conference, but created from the relationships and common cause we’ve built together.
      There are several NP friends who are now podcasting for the common good (Doug Serven
      and Justin Edgar’s I {heart} PCA is a great initiative), these aren’t Partnership things, but my
      hope is that continuing to do the work of polity for the good of our little denomination
      cultivates the ground for Kingdom work. The more we are willing to press forward toward
      health at the Presbytery and denominational level, the greater the harvest in the things we all
      love about laboring in the Church. I’m convinced that it is a trap to rely solely on
      gerrymandering away our conflicts in the PCA through polity. Instead we can be instigators
      for good and take up all the oxygen in the room with a beautiful orthodoxy. That’s our aim. If
      you have friends you want to connect, by all means send me their contact info, but we will
      not be pursuing a GRN-type footprint in the PCA. What we will be looking to do is to continue
      maintaining a healthy path for others to walk.



   3. We will continue to see micro-expressions of anxiety like the Firm Foundation Partnership
      and others. While I’m not concerned about their clout I am concerned that they reveal a need
      to address our online ethics, possibly through a change in our ministerial vows, almost
      certainly through discipline. I’m concerned about ministers in difficult revitalization projects or
      struggling presbyteries who will be forced to defend themselves against hurtful and baseless
      accusations of liberalism from the congregant who saw something online. The kingdom time
      and energy wasted refuting false statements, and the damage done not only to the
      reputations of the slandered but to the slanderers themselves only strengthens the need for
      work on the subject. Other denominations have found a way to work at this, the PCA ought
      to do the same.

I’m far more interested in where we may be headed as a denomination. The AIC report is a great
example of why I’m in the PCA. We have tools at our disposal, and all the necessary resources, to
provide the best answers to the questions our world is asking. I was proud to learn from those on the
committee, and it doesn’t end there. The PCA, I’ve learned through the first two years of the
Beautiful Orthodoxy conference, is full of men and women laboring for the Kingdom in ways that
inspire a sacred imagination. The PCA gets plenty of things wrong, but I’m increasingly convinced
that if there’s a group most capable of getting it right, it’s this group. We have tremendous potential
to serve a global, diverse, orthodox, beautiful church. Campus ministry and foreign mission, church
planting and revitalization, the training up of men and women to serve in ministries that confront the
full range of human needs. One of my favorite authors is fond of saying “it’s our generation’s turn to
be alive.” This generation of ministers, of men and women at work, has been given the responsibility
not only to live but to lead. The fathers and mothers, grandfathers and grandmothers of our
denomination are giving us permission to carry this little corner of the church forward. No manner of
distraction or divisiveness is worth looking aside from our task. We should follow the example set by
our AIC: continue to speak with wisdom, pastoral care, and clear guidance, and lead.
Next week: a typical NP note with polity updates, calendar items, etc.

Dear friends,

Just one item today:

As we begin to plan for next Summer’s General Assembly there is one easy way to begin working
together: we need to understand how committee representatives are populated by our presbyteries.
This isn’t a complicated polity issue but it makes a big difference in the health of our denomination. If
our GA committees (not just the Overtures committee but places like the Nominating Committee) are
not representative of their presbyteries or the denomination as a whole, extreme shifts happen at the
assembly. Some presbyteries have an open nominations process, taking recommendations from the
floor then voting as a body of the whole. Others have a closed system where an administrative
committee votes on a slate and sends them to the floor. In still other presbyteries nominating
commissions will make appointments and report back without the full body voting. In some cases
this has led to some of you wanting to participate on a committee but finding that these
appointments have already been decided far in advance. In my opinion an open nominating process
is best and it may be helpful for us to understand which presbyteries operate according to which
tradition.



To help one another understand how to pursue a nomination for committee work through their
presbytery, I would appreciate you taking just a moment to indicate on this NP spreadsheet
(not shared outside our group) about how your presbytery makes these nominations. Thanks
so much for your help on this!
Link:

https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1QdghA3NVIPM1enWWAo2tPlyu0QpssbmwwdhCySBoEf8
/edit?usp=sharing




Next week: discussion of opportunities to serve in the denomination through committee work.



Thanks all!

JK

to thenp@googlegroups.com

[We ask that you not distribute this email without the permission of the author]

Dear friends,
As we turn toward the business of General Assembly and, hopefully, out of the business of surviving
a pandemic, I wanted to reset our map of things a bit.

First, it will be wise to keep you schedule clear in September as I do believe there's a strong chance
we'll be moving the Assembly back a few months this year. We should know within a month what the
Admin Committee has decided, but given the state of things nationally I am pessimistic that we will
be able to hold GA in June as scheduled. The alternate timeline seems to be September. Just a
heads up for your long-range planning.

Second, since this is technically the 2020 GA postponed a year, the slate of nominations and
committee memberships is carried forward an additional year. Here's how the postponed assembly
affects things for 2021, via advice from our former Stated Clerk, Roy Taylor:

     1. All current members of General Assembly level Committees, Agency Boards, Special
           Committees, and the Standing Judicial Commission will have their terms continue until the
           next General Assembly. The parliamentary principle we are following is that leaders remain
           in office until the end of the Assembly that elects their successors. This practically means
           that all General Assembly terms are extended by one year. Those who were in the class of
           2020 are now in the class of 2021 (still serving until the end of the 2021 General Assembly).
     2. So that there would not be a disruption of the normal class structure of General Assembly
           committees and boards, the orderly consequence of extending the term of those initially
           scheduled for 2020 election is that those in subsequent General Assembly classes will also
           have their terms extended for one year (e.g. those who were in the class of 2023 are now in
           the class of 2024, and so on).
There will not be any new nominations taken for General Assembly positions from your
presbytery this year. The slate put forth by the Nominating Committee from their March 2020
meeting for the postponed General Assembly is still the current slate to be considered for the
upcoming General Assembly. If vacancies arise from that current slate, the Nominating Committee
will fill them with those men already nominated by their presbytery. If other vacancies arise from
resignation or death, they will be handled by floor nomination at General Assembly, as would occur
in our regular General Assembly processes.
Previous presbytery representatives on the Nominating Committee (NC) and Review of
Presbytery Records Committee (RPR) will continue in service – their class years have also been
extended by one year. If your presbytery’s representative was to roll off in 2020, he will now roll off
in 2021. Your presbytery does not need to send in new appointments to these committees this year
unless your current member has moved from your presbytery or no longer wishes to serve.

For Committees of Commissioners – You need take no further action. If you wish to have the
men you previously appointed for the postponed General Assembly represent your presbytery, then
there is no further action for you to take.

Here's what this means for us as we engage in the assembly work this year:
   1. Following the confirmation of the GA meeting dates, you should double-check with your
       presbytery to ensure that your representative to the Overtures Committee wishes to continue
       that appointment. If not, perhaps you or someone else with a healthy view of the
       denomination's mission could take that spot. Just like past years, this Assembly requires the
       work of all of us ensuring that the healthiest vision of the PCA is reflected by her committees.
   2. Once you confirm your presbytery's representative to the OC, would you update our list
       here?

        https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1QdghA3NVIPM1enWWAo2tPlyu0QpssbmwwdhCy
        SBoEf8/edit?usp=sharing



Finally I want to reflect on where we are, and where we’re going.
You all know I've never wanted the NP to see itself in opposition to the GRN, as one political party
against another. The formula for a two-party system's fruitlessness is something we all experience in
the world of national politics. There's a reason for this: not only does this two-party framework
encourage conflict (you must constantly respond to the actions and rhetoric of the other) but it also
constrains creativity and generosity; if the NP sees itself as an opponent of the GRN then it will
waste necessary kingdom energy addressing ideas it does not advocate, instead of moving toward a
greater vision of the Kingdom and marshaling its energies to realize that vision.

At the same time, I am concerned not by the GRN but by the effectiveness of the cultural wedge
issues they continue to utilize as calls to action and times to stand. The past four years the assembly
has been embroiled by rhetoric. We hastily, in my opinion, affirmed the Nashville Statement even as
a far better expression of our orthodoxy was within our grasp. We have normalized an annual
exercise of distrust; sending pugnacious overtures to the OC, punching for the express purpose of
making the "unorthodox" howl. Riding the wave of culture-war, fear has created a strong voting block
that has not only stilted our voice as an Assembly but helped to repopulate some of our committees,
like the SJC, with less healthy expressions of our denominational body. While many have been
focused on how to make our denominational home more hospitable to a Biblically-Orthodox and
beautiful Church that welcomes every tribe, others have barred the door. I'm not sure we realize how
effectively they've done so.

Maybe you have already read Greg Thompson's letter concurring with his presbytery's action to
divest him without censure, but his explanation of why he does not think it would be fruitful to
continue in the PCA is instructive to us. Of course it arises out of a distinct situation, and without
commentary on the rest of his letter and his commendable work, I'll quote him here: "while there are
many, many wonderful individual men and women laboring in our denomination (and you are surely
among them), the culture of our denomination—a culture that is at once instinctively suspicious,
chronically self-righteous, and promiscuously litigious—nurtures an ecclesial climate in which our
chief concern can never truly be the needs of the most vulnerable members of our society, but must
always be the anxieties of the most vocal members of our denomination." [emphasis mine]

The last four years the GRN has worked, in my opinion, to amplify the volume not of the most
vulnerable in our society but the most vocal anxieties in our denomination. Our work, not in
opposition, but in contrast, is important. This is not the most important GA of our lifetime, because
they're all important - we leave the crisis management to God, but this next assembly has the
potential to be particularly fruitful if we heed warnings like Greg's and pursue a beautiful orthodoxy
together.

The PCA continues to grow in a time of church recession. The PCA is where the head of the NAE
(Walter Kim), the president of the Gospel Coalition (Julius Kim), and the Global Executive Director at
Lausanne (Michael Oh) have their membership. The denomination where we are working to promote
equity for minority ministry candidates, where we continue to create the kind of enthusiasm for the
great commission that we send out scores of church planters, campus ministers and leaders, foreign
missionaries into the field. There are great things, precious things, we ought to treasure about what
we've helped to create. If we're going to redirect our concern as pastors and elders toward those
who are longing for the Kingdom of God instead of the most entrenched critics of our collective work,
Greg Thompson is right -- we will need to aim higher than fighting. We will need to tend to some
basic fields of faithfulness to the work of the PCA. I'm optimistic because I believe all of these are
within our reach:

    1. We will have to forge relationships with other pastors and elders in the denomination beyond
       the boundaries of the NP. While I'm not actively recruiting more people into the NP, I do think
       it is possible for the NP to grow in its good influence across the denomination. An example of
       this kind of initiative is David Richter and Walt Nilsson's efforts to forge broader connections
       in the PCA among the various tribes. They're ensuring that a broad coalition of health in the
       PCA will have the ability to act in concert. Not just for crucial votes but to inform our practices
       as gospel witnesses in the world and to build the kind of common cause (call it
       connectionalism if you like) that will help us to pursue good initiatives together rather than as
       confederate presbyteries.



    2. We have to more energetically gather our like-minded friends around the work we do. Do you
       have a group of people who may not be connected to the NP but are connected to you?
       There's no reason you shouldn't share your thoughts with them. I don't mind if you reproduce
       the work we do here. And I certainly don't mind if your ten non-NP PCA friends at GA want to
       work with you to vote in unity on the floor. There's no reason why all of us shouldn't have a
       group of friends we're resourcing during the work of the assembly. Reproducing the NP
       model of honest collaboration among friends at presbytery and the GA is a wise way to look
       out for your fellow pastors and elders who do not have a group like ours. So put together
       your group now and plan ahead for June/September. I'm encouraged that when we arrive at
       the GA we will have a more unified and informed group of voters on the floor, a group that
       knows it not only has a common interest in the various votes but a common cause.



    3. Devote a small portion of each week to denominational work. I'll share my own personal
       practice here, bear with me. My Wednesday afternoons are for non-local church ministry.
       That includes NP issues and the Beautiful Orthodoxy conference and a global church
       planting collaborative, but more broadly it involves studying denominational health. Some
          weeks I run out of time, that's okay. Others I have the space to read overtures and study
          committee reports; I can read ByFaith and pray for the PCA, or participate in calls with
          denominational leaders. I try to create space for this work not because I want to fight but
          because I believe that God has given the PCA a unique opportunity to leverage its unique
          giftedness for the sake of a time where it is desperately needed. And I don't want to look
          back at the end of my career and feel that I did not make use of the moments the Lord gave
          me to bear greater kingdom fruit. Our work in this denomination is one of those opportunities.
          While the vast majority of our heart and tears and hours in the week are dedicated to the
          work of our local church - and it truly delights us - I don't think it's wrong to delight in this
          work, too. I make no apologies for it. When I sit and listen to the stories of the gospel at the
          Beautiful Orthodoxy conference or The Fellowship's yearly gathering, or read about what's
          happening in a corner of our denomination I never knew existed, I thank God that he gives
          me an opportunity to help increase its righteous reach into the world.
One writer talks about her shock when she realized it was her generation's time to live, that the world
was in their care. But it also meant that it was their opportunity to create the beauty, the craft, the
artistry and agony of what it means to be human in that place and in that time. I'm 41. Many of you
are in my age-range. This, frankly, is our generation's time to live as leaders and laborers. What our
little corner of the church accomplishes, our local church and the broader church to which we belong
by God's own hand, is ours to cultivate or ours to curse. I made a deal eight years ago that I would
never whine about my denomination, because I believed I had the opportunity to help lead it. That's
what we've done together. It's not time to fight or time to stand or time to sound the alarm; it's just
time to lead. I want you to know where I stand, and how optimistic I am that the future will be a sight
to see. Whenever the Assembly happens this year, I look forward to seeing you there and pulling on
the same rope.




A statement about the PCA worth having

I. News

II. Overture Prep

III. Calendar



News

Dear friends,

Many of you have heard about the letter drafted by Mike Khandjian and several other contributors. I
was not one of the principal authors, but I am grateful to see it out in the world. I’d commend it to you
as one way that we can help guide a healthier denomination. Like you, I’ve encountered a lot of
exhaustion and raw nerves from the last 15 months of pandemic life piled upon a particularly divisive
cultural moment. My view is that much of the anxiety we’re seeing has no small part to do with the
fact that we have very little margin for charity. Someone has to lead the way in re-asserting our
values and extending charity. This letter moves in that direction. I would encourage you to consider
signing it, as I have, and to share it with your elders/pastors as well. There are some names on that
list who have been real inspirations for me and you, I’m sure. I’d be proud to see our signatures on it,
too.
One of the guys from our group has shared his particular rationale for signing, and encouraging
others to sign.

1.) It's a plea for charity in our discourse

2.) It presents a different narrative about the state of the PCA than some others have offered. There
are whole conferences promoting the idea that the PCA is about to go over a cliff, and we just don't
think that's true at all.

3.) It supports a GFS course for the future of the PCA, as it has served us well so far.



I’d say that about covers it.

One note: I’m sure that some of you may have very good reasons for not signing this letter. As you
know, the NP has always tried to achieve unity rather than uniformity. I want you to know that there is
no lack of respect or grace for those of you who for one reason or another feel you cannot sign it.
Glad to be doing the work together. Here’s the link to the letter:



https://www.afaithfulpca.net




**If you haven’t already signed up for the Fellowship Dinner (Not an NP event but lots of you guys
will be there), here’s the signup: https://chapelgate.ccbchurch.com/goto/forms/873/responses/new?

Among others, like Scott Sauls and our new CTS President Tom Gibbs, I’ll get a chance to promote
the 2022 Beautiful Orthodoxy conference in February (we’ll open registration for the first time at GA).
The Fellowship Dinner is a blast every year and a needed encouragement in a work-heavy week.
Hope you’ll be there.




Overture Prep

Beginning Wednesday, I’ll send out some preliminary thoughts on the overtures before the assembly
this year. We’ll have a chance to discuss via email with the group as in years past. That’ll give us ten
days of prep to discuss any issues and resource one another. My overall view is that while this is a
record-breaking year for assembly attendance, the Overtures Committee will be as evenly divided as
in years past. I’ll share a post-OC updated recommendation as well.

Dear friends,

I wanted to provide an overview of the General Assembly including key meetings, reports, voting
times, etc. Some of this will be subject to change as the assembly begins.

A quick note: If you’re going to be at the Assembly, you really ought to be sure to be present and
voting when necessary. If by some chance you need to schedule something during the business of
the Assembly, do not miss any of the items in red. These are when critical votes and committee
reports happen. Plan accordingly! Every year I hear from commissioners who miss out by not
carefully planning their trips to the coffee station. As we’ve always said in the NP, this is a work trip
and we should be ready to do the work of churchmen. Looking forward to seeing many of you next
week! Next update should happen early in the week of the Assembly, updated overtures advice.
CALENDAR

10a: Overtures briefing and meeting (Rooms 101/102)

GA Seminars of particular interest:

Seeking Unity in the PCA: a Conversation on Subscription With David Strain and David Cassidy
Tuesday June 29 - 3:35-4:20pm in room 274

The PCA is a Good-Faith Subscription denomination that sometimes experiences difficulty with how
we apply that conviction in ways that avoid conflict between various Presbyteries and Elders. The
historical debate between Good-Faith Subscription and Strict Subscription still influences many
people and practices, and occasionally resurfaces with some degree of intensity, as it has again
recently. In this gathering, two Pastors who love the PCA and our Confessional Standards engage in
a conversation about where differences exist on this important issue and look to model how we can
walk together in both fidelity to our Standards and unity of heart and purpose. Please join David
Strain, Pastor of First Presbyterian Church in Jackson, Mississippi and David Cassidy, Pastor of
Spanish River Church in Boca Raton, Florida for their conversation, hosted by their mutual friend
Nate Shurden, Pastor of Cornerstone Church in Franklin, Tennessee.



Above reproach, self-controlled, and holy, or the moral and spiritual qualifications for ordinands:
Exegetical and ethical notes.

Wednesday June 30, 8.00am

Dan Doriani



The Future Glory of the Church: The PCA We Envision for Christ’s Purposes.

Wednesday June 30, 9.15am

Dr. Bryan Chapell will moderate the six-member panel of teaching elders coming from a range of
callings from the pastorate to college presidency, from work in distinctly PCA settings to ecumenical
settings: Michael Allen, academic dean at Reformed Theological Seminary Orlando; Chuck Garriott,
executive director of Ministry to State; Brian Habig, pastor of Downtown Presbyterian Church in
Greenville, South Carolina; Julius Kim, associate pastor of New Life Presbyterian Church and
president of The Gospel Coalition; Philip Ryken, president of Wheaton College; and Thurman
Williams, urban church planter and member of the Mission to North America permanent committee.

Dear friends,



Attached is the initial advice concerning overtures to the 48th GA.

Before you review it, a couple of notes:



1. The NP is not a voting bloc, we do not require you to vote one particular way, though we are
united in purpose as a result of being in this thing together: we want a healthy denomination and we
have a view as to how that looks. Feel free to offer your contrary advice. In fact, our thinking often
benefits from the brilliant dissent, the minority opinion we haven’t considered. If you have it, bring it.



2. The advice contains a column with Dr. David Coffin’s advice. He is not giving that advice as a
member of the NP, of course, but he distributes his opinion widely and because I find him to be
regularly helpful (though we disagree on some points), I thought it would be helpful to include his
thoughts.



3. This is only initial advice, the first of around three versions. The next version will be distributed to
NP representatives to the Overtures committee and will reflect any suggested changes coming from
this list. A third copy will reflect the actions of the OC and will aid you as a resource when the
overtures come to the assembly floor. So don’t fret if something seems half-baked. We’re just
beginning.

to james, NP

James, et al.


Re: O. 40. I read David's response and wondered what kind of overture he would support. As he
said, it is an extraordinarily well prepared overture. It comes from the Study Committee on
Abuse. If the objection is to the "might include procedures such as written testimony...." then a
motion to delete the written testimony part could be made. But as one who worked on this with
the abuse study committee, I don't know what else we can propose. This is what the abuse
study committee wants and sees as necessary.


I am open to any suggestions.


As for O. 41, this also comes from the abuse study committee. Here is an example of the
concern driving this overture: Suppose a congregation has a member who is married to an non-
believer. The non-believing spouse is abused in the marriage. The present 35-1 would open the
door to the non-believing spouse being prohibited from being a witness. We are seeking a
simple remedy by this overture.


Cal Boroughs

to larry.c.hoop, james, NP

As the principal author of Overture 48, I wanted to give some insight into our presbytery's rationale
for writing it.



1) It was written in response to Anti-Asian Racism, especially to the exponential increase in racist
incidents and hate crimes over the past 1 1/2 years. We felt that the PCA has not addressed this
reality adequately. This silence and perceived indifference has not fallen on deaf ears among Asian-
Americans in the PCA.



2) We collaborated with the authors of the Metro Atlanta Overture, #45. Both our presbyteries
wanted to address anti-asian racism but from distinct perspectives that reflected our presbytery's
cultural differences: MAT's majority white culture and KCP's Korean-American culture. In the process
of collaborating, we saw this overture as an extension or application of the Racial
Reconciliation Study Committee Report.
3) I disagree with Coffin's assessment that "In everything that the overture legitimately seeks from a
church court, the PCA is already, boldly, plainly, and Constitutionally committed to." I think every
Asian-American commissioner could tell stories about micro and macro aggressions they've
experienced at GA. Personally, I've been to 7 GA's and have experienced something
offensive/hurtful/cringe-worthy every year that makes me feel like a perpetual outsider in the PCA.
Additionally, the rhetoric that some PCA folks were expressing after the Atlanta shootings and in
response to increased anti-asian racism (use of the phrase Kung Flu, down playing the role of race
in the ATL shootings, whitesplaining Asian-American pain and trauma) shows that more anti-racism
work needs to be done in the PCA, specifically as it applies to anti-Asian racism, which often times
looks very different than anti-black racism.



Many Asian-Americans in the PCA would love for us to pass one of these overtures (45, 46, aor 48).
However, we also know that it's an uphill climb for many reasons, legitimate and also not.
Procedurally, is there a way to amend the overture and/or combine it with overtures 45/46 to make it
more passable without neutering it?




Hansoo Jin

Pastor




brad
<brad@gracewoodlands.org>

Hansoo,



Thanks so much for writing out that explanation. We all mourn as we learn more about the uptick in
anti-Asian racism in our country.
To your question, individual overtures may be amended in the Overtures Committee. But the
resulting amended overture must pass the test of germaneness. In other words, you can't change it
to a completely different overture. Once one overture passes, the Assembly can answer the other
one in reference to the first one.



I'm not sure exactly what we can do to the overture to give it a better chance at passing. Maybe we
could put together a small working group of folks to hone this?



If you recall, the first time an overture of repentance for our failures during the Civil Rights era was
attempted it did not pass. But the fact that we were able to bring it to the floor for discussion allowed
many to speak to the issue and resulted in Jim Baird's moving speech. It was not until the next year,
after the overture was perfected, that it passed with a 90% vote.

Rev. Dr. Timothy R. LeCroy

Dear friends,

confidentiality in this group. You’ll notice I haven’t included the confidentiality notice atop the email.
The reason is that our last overtures advice email was shared by one of us with TE Todd Pruitt and
TE Chuck Williams. Both of whom decided to share that email within a FB group of PCA pastors
which numbers more than 500. I can only assume the email was shared even more broadly.

Now, I have said it before and I’ll say it again: the intent of confidentiality was always to protect those
of you who felt you could not be as forthcoming in larger groups. I’ve always wanted the NP to be a
place where you can seek advice with confidence that your questions weren’t being used to fuel blog
posts. The lack of confidentiality makes no difference in what I share with you. Emails will say pretty
much what they would have said. But for those of you nervous about emails to the group, please
know that you are welcome to reply only to me and I’ll do my best to respond as I’m able. Looking
forward to seeing you all next week. Praying for a God-glorifying five days in STL.

to NP

{This email is not to be shared without permission from the sender}
Friends,

I asked an RPR expert, Tim Lecroy, to give an overview of the action on the RPR report slated
for 11a today. My own view is in line with the RPR. While we must take care to not spread teaching
hostile to our system of doctrine as ruled by our presbyteries, the PCA is not a denomination that
forbids the teaching of all differences with the standards.

_________



There is one minority report for Calvary and I suspect that NW Georgia will be pulled out of the
omnibus for the same issue.



RPR Report

There will be at least one and possibly two recommendations of the RPR report that will receive
debate this morning. The first one will be the recommendation for Calvary Presbytery. The
underlying issue is that the Presbytery put a total ban on a man teaching his views on his stated
difference to the Westminster Standards even though they ruled that the difference was not out of
accord with our system of doctrine. This violates BCO Preliminary Principle 7, which states that "no
church judiciary may make laws to bind the conscience." The 46th GA in 2018 ruled that this action
by Calvary Presbytery was an exception of substance, meaning that their action was contrary to the
constitution of the PCA.



 Calvary came back to the GA in 2019 with a response that basically said, "no, the GA is wrong
about that." In 2019 the 27th GA ruled again that Calvary Presbytery had interpreted the constitution
of the church wrongly and their response was unacceptable. In 2020, Calvary presbytery sent back
the same exact response as 2019, saying again that the GA was wrong. This year the RPR is
recommending that the matter be referred to the SJC for final disposition per RAO 16-10.c. The
basic reason behind this is because, while the GA has authority to interpret the constitution in
General Review and Control, it does not currently have the ability to bring the matter to final
disposition. The SJC does have that authority, as delegated to it by the GA. Thus instead of
continuing this back and forth between the GA and Calvary Presbytery, the RPR is recommending
the matter be referred to the SJC. The vote on RPR was 36-9-0. There will be a minority report.



The issue for NW Georgia is almost identical, if that one gets pulled out of the omnibus.

--

Rev. Dr. Timothy R. LeCroy

My thoughts on O23
22 views

                                                 



james
<james@newcityhilliard.org>

Jul 1, 2021, 1:21:27 PM (8 days ago)
                                                           
                                                           
                                                           
to NP

{This communication is private and is not to be shared without the permission of the sender}



Friends,

As we enter into the latter parts of Thursday's assembly we will begin debating the human sexuality
overtures. I wanted to give my rationale for my support of Overture 23 as things currently stand. I
have watched many of you cautioning against the adoption of the overture, and have had several
conversations along these lines. I've tried to pay close attention to your arguments to see if there are
places the Lord might be persuading me differently. So far, this is where I come out on the issue:



The language:
Officers in the Presbyterian Church in America must be above reproach in their walk and Christlike in their character.
Those who profess an identity (such as, but not limited to, “gay Christian,” “homosexual Christian,” same sex
attracted Christian,” or like term) that undermines or contradicts their identity as new creations in Christ, or deny the
sinfulness of fallen desires (such as, but not limited to, same sex attraction), or deny the reality and hope of
progressive sanctification, or fail to pursue Spirit-empowered victory over their sinful temptations, inclinations, and
actions are not qualified for ordained office.

(1) My greatest concern at the moment is that there is no specific language to which a SSA minister
can point in our BCO that protects them from non-ordination or dismissal based on sharing their
story of SSA. This overture provides for a qualified support of identification language, by defining the
identification language that is not appropriate. It would be the now-enumerated right of someone with
SSA to ordination in the PCA if their self-identification does not prevent them from assenting to the
orthdox views of sanctification.

(2) The troubling "reputation" language of the original overture, combined with the vagueness of
"identity" language, are the horns on which we currently hang our SSA brothers in the ordination
process. It's what we are doing now. This overture allows us to bypass that conversation entirely.
Reputation is not listed here, and self-profession is defined not by some kind of clumsy biblicism but
by our confessional standards on the doctrine of sanctification.

(3) The overture provides process for the ordinand who is unlawfully prevented from ordination due
to self-identification or other terminology. They have a specific provision to cite and a means to
appeal.


Many of you have talked about how this all reads to our friends who struggle with this sort of sexual
brokenness, that it elevates their sexual brokenness above our own, breaking our fidelity to the
doctrines of grace. I can certainly understand that. Furthermore I find myself nodding vigorously
every time our friends tell us that these discussions are never to or for them. It's incredibly uncaring,
the process we've undertaken. I agree. I intend to make that clear in my floor speech.
It is my recognition of the kind of suffering some of you, and your friends, have encountered in the
courts of the denomination that compel me to try and begin to put a stop to it. Simply voting them all
down I do not believe will accomplish much of note. We will all go home and many of us will be
distracted again with ministry and everyday life, while those of you struggling through witch-hunts
due to your self-profession will have to fight with a much smaller posse, the fight you've been fighting
all along. I can't stand to leave things there, if I can help it.

I'm open to counter-arguments, and I know acutely that my current position on this is a
disappointment to quite a few of you. Forgive me if I haven't listened well, or if you feel I've
dismissed you in any way. If I'm wrong, extend to me the grace of being wrong too, from time to time.
Hopefully God will grant us wisdom beyond Solomon for the discussion to come.




travis
<travis@graceandpeacepgh.org>

Jul 1, 2021, 2:05:23 PM (8 days ago)

                                                           
